decitabine and cedazuridine
INQOVI
nucleoside metabolic inhibitor (decitabine)
cytidine deaminase inhibitor (cedazuridine)
Astex Pharmaceuticals, Inc.
Tablet
One INQOVI (35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine) tablet
taken orally once daily on Days 1 through 5 of each 28-day
cycle
INQOVI is a combination of cedazuridine, a cytidine deaminase
inhibitor, and decitabine, a nucleoside metabolic inhibitor,
indicated for treatment of adult patients with myelodysplastic
syndromes (MDS) including previously treated and untreated,
de novo and secondary MDS of all French-American-British
subtypes (refractory anemia, refractory anemia with ringed
sideroblasts, refractory anemia with excess blasts, refractory
anemia with excess blasts in transformation, and chronic
myelomonocytic leukemia) and intermediate-1, intermediate-2,
and high-risk International Prognostic Scoring System groups
Regular approval

Recommendation on
Regulatory Action
Recommended INQOVI is a combination of decitabine, a nucleoside metabolic
Indication(s)/Population(s) inhibitor, and cedazuridine, a cytidine deaminase inhibitor,
(if applicable) indicated for treatment of adult patients with myelodysplastic
syndromes (MDS) including previously treated and untreated,
de novo and secondary MDS with the following French­

Prognostic Scoring System groups.

1 Executive Summary
Product Introduction
Trade name:
Established name:
Also known as:
Dosage form:
Therapeutic class:
Chemical class:
Pharmacologic class:
Mechanism of action:

Inqovi®
decitabine and cedazuridine
ASTX727
Tablets: 35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine
Antineoplastic
Small molecule
nucleoside metabolic inhibitor (decitabine),
cytidine deaminase inhibitor (cedazuridine)
hypomethylating agent (decitabine) is antineoplastic agent

The Applicant submitted NDA 212576 for decitabine/cedazuridine tablet, also known as
ASTX727, for the treatment of patients with previously treated and untreated myelodysplastic
syndrome (MDS) and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML). Decitabine is a
hypomethylating agent approved as the intravenous formulation for the same indication sought
by the Applicant. Cedazuridine is a new molecular entity (NME) and is a cytidine deaminase
inhibitor that prevents the rapid metabolism of decitabine in the gastrointestinal tract to allow
for oral administration. The NDA provides for a fixed dose combination tablets of 35 mg of
decitabine and 100 mg of cedazuridine which when given once daily for 5 days provides
comparable cumulative exposure to the IV decitabine 5-day regimen of 20 mg/m2 by
intravenous infusion over 1 hour daily for 5 days. Decitabine/cedazuridine fixed dose tablet is
administered one tablet daily on days 1-5 of a 28-day cycle until disease progression or
unacceptable toxicity.

Conclusions on the Substantial Evidence of Effectiveness
The review teams conclude that the information submitted by the Applicant has provided
substantial evidence for the effectiveness of decitabine/cedazuridine for the treatment of adult
patients with previously treated and untreated myelodysplastic syndrome (MDS) and chronic
myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML) and supports regular approval of this indication. This
approval was based on the results of a phase 1/2 study (ASTX727-01) and a randomized phase 3
study (ASTX727-02). The recommendation is based on demonstration of equivalent exposure
over 5 days and no erosion of efficacy compared to the 5-day IV decitabine dosing. The
geometric mean ratio (90% CI) for the 5-day cumulative decitabine exposure between
decitabine/cedazuridine and IV decitabine was 0.99 (0.93, 1.06). In the phase 2 study, the
complete response (CR) rate was 18% (95% CI: 10, 28) with duration of response of 8.7 months
(range: 1.1, 18.2). In the phase 3 study, the CR rate was 21% (95% CI: 15, 29) with duration of
response of 7.5 months (range: 1.7, 17.5).

INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)

Benefit-Risk Assessment (BRA)
Benefit-Risk Summary and Assessment
Decitabine and cedazuridine tablets, also known as ASTX727, are a fixed-dose combination drug product that contains the active ingredients
decitabine (35 mg) and cedazuridine (100 mg). Decitabine is a hypomethylating agent approved as intravenous formulation for the treatment
of myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML). The oral bioavailability of decitabine is limited by
degradation of decitabine by cytidine deaminase (CDA) enzyme activity present in the gastrointestinal tract and liver of humans. Cedazuridine
is a CDA inhibitor, and oral administration of cedazuridine with decitabine enhances the oral bioavailability of decitabine. The proposed dose of
decitabine and cedazuridine tablets provides systemic decitabine exposure comparable to the approved dose of intravenous decitabine.
MDS is a heterogeneous group of diseases characterized by ineffective, dysplastic hematopoiesis leading to cytopenias. Clinical presentation of
MDS is a manifestation of cytopenias including fatigue, infections, and bleeding and risk of transformation to acute myeloid leukemia (AML)
which is rapidly fatal without treatment. Patients with MDS have an increased risk of mortality. Current prognostic classification is the is the
International Prognostic Scoring System-Revised (IPSS-R), and higher-risk MDS is classified as IPSS-R intermediate- (score >3.5), high-, or very
high-risk MDS. Higher-risk MDS can be treated in the frontline setting with hypomethylating agents, azacitidine or decitabine. CMML is a
related hematopoietic disorder characterized by peripheral blood monocytosis. CMML was previously classified as MDS and is currently
classified as myelodysplastic/myeloproliferative neoplasm (MDS/MPN), and the treatment approach is similar to that for MDS.
Decitabine was approved as the intravenous formulation in 2006 for treatment of adult patients with previously treated and untreated, de
novo and secondary MDS and CMML. In clinical studies leading to the approval of intravenous decitabine, the overall response rate (ORR) was
16-17% with complete response (CR) rate of 9-15%.
Because of extensive clinical experience with intravenous decitabine, the development of ASTX727 was designed such that oral administration
of ASTX727 over 5 days would result in comparable exposure to intravenous decitabine administered daily for 5 days. The clinical program
would then provide evidence of similar safety and efficacy profile compared to intravenous decitabine. The demonstration of comparable
exposure, no erosion of efficacy, and similar safety profile was based on a phase 2 study (ASTX727-01-B) and a phase 3 study (ASTX727-02) in
adult patients with MDS and CMML, for which intravenous decitabine is currently approved. Both studies were randomized, open-label, 2­
sequence crossover studies where patients received either oral ASTX727 or intravenous decitabine in cycles 1 and 2 with two-way crossover,
18
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
and the remaining cycles were oral ASTX727. The primary endpoints for each study were 5-day cumulative exposure. Secondary endpoints
included safety and efficacy including overall response rate and rates of transfusion independence. The acceptable response rate for MDS
appropriate for regulatory decision-making is ORR defined as CR+PR responses. There were no PR responses on the Phase 2 or Phase 3 trials of
ASTX727, and therefore, only CR responses were considered for demonstration of clinical benefit.
The geometric mean ratio for the 5-day cumulative decitabine exposure between decitabine/cedazuridine and IV decitabine was 0.99 (90% CI:
0.93, 1.06) in the phase 3 study. In study ASTX727-01-B in 80 patients, the CR rate was 18% (95% CI: 10, 28) with duration of response of 8.7
months (range: 1.1, 18.2). In study ASTX727-02 in 133 patients, the CR rate was 21% (95% CI: 15, 29) with duration of response of 7.5 months
(range: 1.7, 17.5). Notably, the initial results from ASTX727-02 did not provide sufficient time on study resulting in low response rates and
relatively short durability; however, a later data cutoff included at the 120-day safety update provided longer follow up. The CR responses
were sufficiently durable to demonstrate clinical benefit in this patient population. The durability was slightly lower than with IV decitabine,
however, the time on treatment was shorter and many of the responses were ongoing at the time of data cutoff, and thus, the median
duration of CR would be expected to increase with longer follow-up. The CR benefit was supported by a transfusion benefit on both trials,
which demonstrated that 49-53% of patients who were dependent on transfusions at baseline achieved at least 56 days transfusion
independence post-baseline and 63-64% of patients who were independent of transfusions at baseline were able to maintain that transfusion
independence for at least 56 days post-baseline.
Safety data was pooled across studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02 given the similar study designs and patient populations. Comparative
review of safety between ASTX727 and IV decitabine was focused in the first cycle of therapy to avoid the possibility of overlapping toxicities
between the first and second cycles of therapy. Evaluation of safety across all cycles included one cycle of IV decitabine, but because adverse
reactions cannot be easily attributed to one cycle, the IV decitabine cycle was included in safety analysis. Overall, the adverse reactions appear
to be more common in patients treated with ASTX727 compared to IV decitabine in the first cycle of therapy. This observation may have been
due to imbalances in baseline demographics. It will be important for clinicians to closely monitor patients while on this oral form of decitabine
given the possibility for significant cytopenias leading to increased risk of infection and bleeding. Despite this being an oral therapy that will
allow patients to spend less time in the clinic, it will be important to have close follow-up for laboratory testing and clinical monitoring. The
important risks of myelosuppression and embryo-fetal toxicity are unchanged from IV decitabine based on the data presented in the
submission. Overall, given the historically poor outcomes of patients with MDS who are candidates for hypomethylating agent therapy, the
level of toxicity observed with ASTX727 is acceptable for the clinical benefit observed.

Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
In conclusion, the submitted clinical information from studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02 provided data for equivalent 5-day exposure of
decitabine when administered as 35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine tablets compared to the 5-day regimen of intravenous decitabine.
Similar efficacy was demonstrated by CR rate and duration of CR supported by transfusion independence. The safety profile was also similar
between oral ASTX727 and intravenous decitabine with small increase in myelosuppression which is manageable with monitoring and dose
modification, if needed. While the treatment regimen evaluated included one cycle of IV decitabine for each patient, treatment with
decitabine typically provides responses over several cycles of therapy, and the single IV cycle likely did not specifically contribute to efficacy of
the oral regimen. Administering all cycles as oral decitabine and cedazuridine tablets is expected to have similar safety and effectiveness as
intravenous decitabine. This provides for an oral rote of administration of decitabine which may reduce time in clinic, and it will be important
for prescribers to monitor compliance and adverse events closely.

2 Therapeutic Context
Analysis of Condition
The Applicant’s Position:
Myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) include a heterogeneous group of hematopoietic stem cell
disorders characterized by dysplastic changes in myeloid, erythroid, and megakaryocytic
progenitors and associated with cytopenias affecting one or more of the three lineages
(Steensma 2018). Median age at diagnosis is approximately 70 years, and prognosis is generally
poor with median survival times of 1.2 and 0.4 years in patients with intermediate (Int)-2 and
high-risk MDS, respectively (Steensma 2018; Greenberg et al 1997). The disease affects more
males than females, and among different racial groups, White individuals have the highest
incidence (Garcia-Manero 2015; Ma et al 2007). MDS may evolve into acute myeloid leukemia
(AML) in one-third of patients (Shukron et al 2012).
Chronic myelomonocytic leukemia (CMML) is characterized by a persistent peripheral blood
monocytosis ≥1×109/L with monocytes accounting for ≥10% of white blood cells (WBCs) (Solary
and Itzykson 2017). The condition is currently categorized by the World Health Organization
(WHO) as a myelodysplastic/ myeloproliferative neoplasm (MDS/MPN) (Arber et al 2016).
However, as CMML was previously classified as MDS, the treatment approach is similar to that
for MDS.
According to the US-based Surveillance, Epidemiology, and End Results (SEER) program, a total
of 28,138 new cases of MDS were diagnosed between 2012-2016 in the US, corresponding to
an age-adjusted incidence rate of 4.5 per 100,000 persons. This estimate excluded a total of
2,961 cases of CMML, which corresponds to a CMML-specific age-adjusted incidence rate of
0.5 per 100,000. Between 2012 and 2016, 90% of cases in the US were diagnosed in patients
≥60 years of age (Howlader et al 2019).
Patients with MDS are at risk of complications of cytopenia and AML transformation. Functional
defects or other comorbidities, such as cardiac or pulmonary conditions, may increase the risk
of complications of anemia, iron overload due to transfusion, or bleeding due to
thrombocytopenia (Steensma 2018). The main prognostic classification system for MDS is the
International Prognostic Scoring System (IPSS), which was introduced in 1997 (Greenberg et al
1997) and revised in 2012 (IPSS-Revised [IPSS-R]) (Greenberg et al 2012). Both systems are
widely used. The IPSS-R uses slightly different cytopenia cutoff points and incorporates the MDS
cytogenetic score. Using IPSS criteria, expected median survival for Int-1, Int-2, and high risk
MDS are 3.5, 1.2, and 0.4 years, respectively (Greenberg et al 1997). Using the comparable risk

25
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
groups in the IPSS-R, the expected median survival for intermediate, high risk, and very high risk
groups are 3, 1.6, and 0.8 years, respectively (Greenberg et al 2012).
There is no universally adopted risk classification system for CMML, but several classifications
recognized the prognostic importance of total WBCs, thus differentiating dysplastic CMML
(lower WBCs) from proliferative CMML (higher WBCs). More recently, the 2016 WHO
classification defined 3 groups (CMML-0; CMML-1; and CMML-2) based on percentage blasts in
peripheral blood and bone marrow (Arber et al 2016). Patients with CMML are generally
diagnosed between age 65 and 75 years with a 2:1 male predominance (Parikh and Tefferi
2012). Similar to MDS, the main manifestations are those of the underlying cytopenia, including
fatigue and dyspnea due to anemia, susceptibility to infections, and bleeding (although less
common). Median survival ranges from approximately 9 to 20 months (Parikh and Tefferi 2012).
In conclusion, both MDS and CMML are serious and life-threatening myeloid malignancies with
short median survival and life-threatening complications due to cytopenias and risk of
transformation to AML.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA agrees that both MDS and CMML are serious diseases with a substantial risk of mortality.

Analysis of Current Treatment Options
The only FDA-approved treatments for Intermediate and high-risk MDS and CMML are the
hypomethylating agents azacitidine (Vidaza, FDA approved in 2004) and decitabine (Dacogen,
FDA approved in 2006) (Table 1). Lenalidomide (Revlimid) is only approved in transfusiondependent anemia in patients with low or intermediate-1 risk MDS with 5q deletion so it is not
of relevance for patients with higher risk MDS or CMML.
Decitabine (Dacogen®) is administered as a 3-day regimen (15 mg/m2 over a 3-hour intravenous
[IV] infusion repeated every 8 hours for 3 days) or a 5-day regimen (20 mg/m2 over a 1-hour IV
infusion repeated daily for 5 days) (Dacogen USPI 2019). Based on published data, the 5-day
regimen has been globally adopted as the standard regimen due to its improved convenience,
better tolerability, lower total cycle dose (100 mg/m2 compared with 135 mg/m2), and
comparable benefit-risk profile compared with the 3-day regimen. Almost all current clinical
studies of IV decitabine use the 5 day regimen. Azacitidine (Vidaza®) is administered daily for
7 days (starting dose of 75 mg/m2 subcutaneous [SC] or IV) (Vidaza USPI 2018). Both drugs have
improved the outcomes of intermediate- and high-risk MDS patients with a median survival
time of 14 to 24 months (Kantarjian et al 2006; Fenaux et al 2009; Zeidan et al 2018).

Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
The Applicant’s Position:
Within the previous 15 years, agents approved for treatment of higher-risk MDS and CMML
(decitabine, azacitidine) have improved patient outcomes (Kantarjian et al 2006, Fenaux et al
2009) with higher response rates, hematological improvement, and higher transfusion
independence than either best supportive care, or other conventional care regimens.
Azacitidine also showed improved survival over conventional care regimens (Fenaux et al 2009).
This is believed to be related to the prolonged treatment (median of 9 azacitidine cycles) in the
AZA-001 study where azacitidine was given as long as the patient continues to benefit (Fenaux
et al 2009). Decitabine MDS studies suffered from a design flaw where decitabine was only
given for 2 cycles after achieving CR, or discontinued if a patient did not achieve at least partial
response (PR) after 6 cycles. This resulted in a much shorter number of cycles in the decitabine
study (median 3 cycles) (Kantarjian et al 2006). It is now globally acknowledged that both
agents should be given continuously since stopping treatment is associated with quick relapse
(Cabrero et al 2015 ), and that patients with stable disease and no objective response can still
have a survival benefit (Silverman et al 2011; Papageorgiou et al 2018). Both Dacogen and
Vidaza USPIs recommend treatment for at least 4 to 6 cycles, and that treatment continues as
long as the patient benefits.
The importance of treatment continuation for several months and sometimes years as long as
the patient is benefitting results in significant burden on MDS patients. Patients with MDS are
often over the age of 70 years and frequently have comorbidities or limited mobility that hinder
their ability to attend daily hospital/clinic visits for multiple days each month to receive IV
infusions or large volume subcutaneous injections, which can extend over several months or
years, particularly in responding patients. This burden may ultimately lead to premature
treatment discontinuation and put patients at risk of relapse, since HMA treatment must be
continued for as long as the patient continues to benefit. At the same time complication of IV
and subcutaneous administrations may result in infections and/or bleeding complications that
may be serious in the context of severe neutropenia and thrombocytopenia that these patients
often have.
An orally available hypomethylating agent (HMA) should substantially reduce the burden of
treatment for patients and their caregivers. Identification of an orally bioavailable HMA agent
has been challenging, primarily due to the rapid metabolism of nucleosides by cytidine
deaminase (CDA) in the gastrointestinal (GI) mucosa and liver. High levels of CDA in the GI tract
and liver rapidly degrade nucleosides upon oral administration and limit or prohibit their oral
bioavailability. For example, to achieve even modest exposures of azacitidine requires
administration of large doses, which are associated with Grade 3 and Grade 4 GI toxicity (e.g.,
nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea) and high variability in systemic exposures (Garcia-Manero et al
2011). The applicant believes that an oral HMA that provides equivalent systemic exposure to
its parenteral form will alleviate the significant burden on MDS patients and their caregivers,
and reduce utilization of hospital/clinic resources needed for parenteral administration. It also
has the potential of achieving better long term treatment compliance, and fewer adverse
29
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
reactions related to multiple parenteral administrations.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA agrees that current treatment options for MDS include decitabine, azacitidine, and
lenalidomide, but that lenalidomide is only approved for patients with transfusion-dependent
anemia due to low- or intermediate-1-risk MDS associated with a deletion 5q abnormality with
or without additional cytogenetic abnormalities. Both decitabine and azacitidine are approved
for patients with MDS, with slight differences in their indication statements, as outlined by the
Applicant above. Of note, the azacitidine indication for the FAB subtypes of RA and RARS is
limited to patients with neutropenia or thrombocytopenia or requiring transfusions, whereas
this is not mentioned in the decitabine indication. Furthermore, the decitabine indication notes
the inclusion of patients with de novo and secondary MDS, whereas this is not mentioned in the
azacitidine indication statement. For decitabine, intermediate-1, intermediate-2, and high-risk
IPSS is stipulated, whereas this is not mentioned in the indication statement for azacitidine.
Lastly, the decitabine indication includes previously treated and untreated patients given that a
subset of patients on the supportive trials received previous therapy for their MDS.
In practice, despite the small differences in the wording of the drug labels, both drugs are used
interchangeably in the frontline setting for the treatment of patients with MDS, predominately
in patients with higher-risk MDS (IPSS-R intermediate [score > 3.5], high, or very high or IPSS
intermediate-2 or high risk) based on NCCN guidelines (NCCN 2020).

3 Regulatory Background
U.S. Regulatory Actions and Marketing History
The Applicant’s Position:
ASTX727 is not currently marketed in the United States. ASTX727 fixed-dose combination (FDC)
tablets are composed of 100 mg cedazuridine and 35 mg decitabine. Cedazuridine is a new
molecular entity (NME). IV decitabine has been FDA approved for intermediate and high-risk
MDS and CMML since 2006, and approved in the EU for previously untreated AML not
candidates for intensive chemotherapy since 2012.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA confirms the Applicant’s assessment.

5 Nonclinical Pharmacology/Toxicology
Executive Summary
ASTX727 is an oral fixed-dose combination (FDC) drug product that contains the active
ingredients decitabine and cedazuridine (E7727). Decitabine (Dacogen®) for injection is an
approved nucleoside metabolic inhibitor; the oral bioavailability of decitabine is limited by
cytidine deaminase (CDA) activity present in the gastrointestinal (GI) tract and liver of humans.
CDA enzymatically deaminates cytidine analogs to generate pharmacologically inactive
metabolites. Substrates of CDA include the cytidine analogs decitabine and gemcitabine.
Cedazuridine is a CDA inhibitor, and oral administration of cedazuridine with decitabine
enhances the oral bioavailability of decitabine. The proposed dose of ASTX727 provides
systemic decitabine exposure comparable to the approved dose of decitabine for injection.
While cedazuridine is not an anti-tumor drug, the combination of decitabine and cedazuridine
as an FDC drug product is indicated to treat patients with cancer; thus, the nonclinical
development program of cedazuridine will follow the recommendations in ICH S9 and ICH S9
Questions and Answers. A complete nonclinical program for cedazuridine is not necessary
because of the combination and known toxicities associated with decitabine, for which the
Applicant has the right of reference.
Cedazuridine inhibited the activity of human CDA in vitro (IC 50 =0.281 μM) and improved the in
vitro half-life of gemcitabine in the presence of human CDA from t 1/2 < 36 minutes to t 1/2 > 66
hours. Cedazuridine demonstrated negligible anti-proliferative activity in vitro at concentrations
up to 10 µM. The in vivo effect of cedazuridine on the antitumor activity of orally administered
decitabine was assessed in the L1210 and KG-1 disseminated leukemia mouse models. In both
mouse tumor models the dose of cedazuridine was varied (1 to 100 mg/kg, oral) while a fixed
dose of decitabine was administered (0.3 or 0.4 mg/kg, oral). Control arms for oral cedazuridine
monotherapy, oral decitabine monotherapy, and intravenous (IV) decitabine monotherapy
were included. Decitabine was administered 30-40 minutes after cedazuridine to allow timedelay inhibition of CDA; contrariwise, cedazuridine and decitabine are delivered simultaneously
in the ASTX727 FDC drug product. In both mouse tumor models oral cedazuridine administered
in combination with oral decitabine conferred an increase in life span (31% to 88%, relative to
vehicle control), but the effect was dose-dependent only in the L1210 disseminated tumor
model. Neither oral cedazuridine nor oral decitabine monotherapy resulted in an increase in life
span relative to vehicle control.
There were no cardiovascular or respiratory findings in a single-dose standalone safety
pharmacology study in cynomolgus monkeys with oral doses of cedazuridine up to 200 mg/kg.
In a 13-week toxicity study, cynomolgus monkeys were orally administered repeated doses of
cedazuridine at up to 200 mg/kg/day with no remarkable electrocardiograph (ECG) findings.
Neither cedazuridine nor its metabolites cross the blood brain barrier. No safety pharmacology
35
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
studies were conducted with ASTX727 or decitabine.
The intended pharmacologic effect of cedazuridine is to improve the oral bioavailability of
decitabine; accordingly, the Applicant conducted a series of studies to evaluate the absorption,
distribution, metabolism, and excretion (ADME) profiles of cedazuridine, decitabine, and
ASTX727. Toxicokinetics (TK) were also evaluated as part of the general toxicology studies for
cedazuridine (in mice and cynomolgus monkeys) and decitabine (in rats). Orally administered
cedazuridine was rapidly absorbed in both species with a plasma T max of ~1 hour in mice and ~2
hours in cynomolgus monkeys. In a time-delay study in cynomolgus monkeys 2-hour
pretreatment with oral cedazuridine greatly enhanced exposure to subsequent orally
administered decitabine (up to 17-fold increase in AUC 0-t , relative to oral decitabine
monotherapy); concomitant oral administration of cedazuridine and decitabine resulted in a
6.4-fold increase in decitabine AUC 0-t , relative to oral decitabine monotherapy.
The distribution, metabolism, and excretion profiles of cedazuridine were evaluated in mass
balance studies in mice and cynomolgus monkeys. In mice, orally administered
[14C]-cedazuridine distributed primarily into the metabolic/excretory and GI tissues and did not
pass the blood brain barrier. The metabolite profile of [14C]-cedazuridine was similar in mice
and cynomolgus monkeys, which primarily consisted of unmodified cedazuridine and
cedazuridine epimer, followed by less abundant metabolites resultant of dehydrogenation,
oxidation, and urea conjugation. All significant human metabolites of cedazuridine were
detected in mice and/or monkeys. In both mice and cynomolgus monkeys, the excretion profile
of orally administered cedazuridine was primarily fecal, and some fecal excretion could be
attributed to unabsorbed drug. In the general toxicology studies, cedazuridine (mice and
cynomolgus monkeys) and decitabine (rats) were administered orally once daily for 5 to 7 days
in 28-day cycles, similar to the clinical regimen. There were no sex-related differences in PK
parameters or significant accumulation of cedazuridine or decitabine.
A series of studies were conducted to assess the toxicity of cedazuridine or decitabine
monotherapy, or cedazuridine in combination with decitabine. A literature assessment
submitted by the Applicant concluded that humans, mice, and cynomolgus monkeys have
similar CDA tissue distribution and activity profiles, and CDA activity was notably absent in rats.
The Applicant concluded mice and cynomolgus monkeys were the appropriate species to
evaluate the toxicity of cedazuridine. Rats were chosen to evaluate the toxicity of oral
decitabine given the relatively high oral bioavailability in this species. The pivotal studies were
conducted under GLP-compliance, and the test articles were administered by the oral route
following dosing regimens comparable to the planned clinical treatment cycle of 5 consecutive
dosing days followed by 23 non-dosing days. Cedazuridine monotherapy was assessed in
repeat-dose toxicity studies up to 13-weeks/4-cycles in duration in mice (up to 1000
mg/kg/dose) and cynomolgus monkeys (up to 200 mg/kg/dose). Findings in mice included bone
marrow cytology changes, non-adverse changes in hematology parameters, increase in spleen
36
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
weight, and histopathologic changes in the bone marrow and spleen (hematopoiesis), small
intestine (hyperplasia), and male (bilateral oligospermia of the epididymis) and female
(follicular degeneration of the ovary) reproductive tracts. All findings were reversible following
a 3-week recovery period; the testicular findings were partially recovered with a reduction in
severity following the recovery period. Findings in cynomolgus monkeys were more limited and
included bone marrow cytology changes, changes in hematology parameters (particularly red
blood cell parameters), and histopathologic changes in the bone marrow (atypia) and GI tract
(mitoses/single cell death and atypia), all of which were reversible following a 3-week recovery
period.
Pivotal studies of oral decitabine monotherapy were limited to a 13-week/4-cycle study
conducted in rats only (up to 7.5 mg/kg/dose). Observations in rats included changes in
hematology parameters, bone marrow cytology changes, decreased thymus, spleen, testis and
epididymis weights, and degenerative histopathological findings in the bone marrow, thymus,
spleen, intestines, and male reproductive organs. Following the 3-week recovery period
findings were reversible; the changes in the male reproductive organs persisted but showed
reduced incidence and severity. The findings for orally administered decitabine were consistent
with the known toxicological profile of IV administered decitabine.
The toxicologic profiling of cedazuridine in combination with decitabine was limited to a nonGLP 4-week/1-cycle study in cynomolgus monkeys. This study did not evaluate the ASTX727
FDC drug product; instead, monkeys were orally administered separate gelatin capsules
containing cedazuridine (1, 3, or 6 mg/kg/day) and decitabine (2 mg/kg/day). This study did not
identify any novel findings. TK analyses identified increased systemic exposure to decitabine
with increasing doses of cedazuridine. Additional combination studies were not conducted due
to the disparate toxic dose levels of decitabine and cedazuridine.
Cedazuridine was evaluated in a standard battery of GLP-compliant genotoxicity studies.
Genetic toxicity studies for cedazuridine were not requested by the FDA because of the
combination therapy that included a genotoxic drug. Cedazuridine was mutagenic in the Ames
assay and genotoxic in the in vitro chromosome aberration assay, but not genotoxic in the in
vivo micronucleus assay. The genotoxic potential of cedazuridine and decitabine will be
described in the product label for ASTX727. Carcinogenicity studies were not conducted and are
not warranted to support a marketing application for the proposed indication.
No stand-alone reproductive and developmental toxicology studies were conducted with
cedazuridine or ASTX727. Decitabine is teratogenic, fetotoxic, and embryotoxic based on its
mechanism of action and animal studies described in the product label for decitabine for
injection; these risks will be described in the product label for ASTX727. In the repeat-dose
toxicity study in mice cedazuridine caused adverse effects in male (bilateral oligospermia of the
epididymis) and female (follicular degeneration of the ovary) reproductive tracts, but these
were observed at exposures >100-fold the human exposures. A mouse fertility study described

5.3. Pharmacology
Primary pharmacology
The Regulatory Authority's Assessment:
Cedazuridine inhibited the recombinant human CDA-catalyzed deamination of cytidi ne in vitro
(ICso=0.281 µM). High levels of cedazuridine (0.39 mM) increased the in vitro half-life of
gemcitabine in the presence of human CDA from t 112 < 36 minutes to t i12 > 66 hours; neither
the effect of clinically-relevant levels of cedazuridine on the in vitro half-life of gemcitabine nor
the effect of cedazuridine on the in vitro half-life of decitabine were assessed. A literature
assessment submitted by the Applicant concluded that among common species used in
nonclinical testing, mice and cynomolgus monkeys have CDA tissue distribution and activity
profiles most similar to humans. CDA activity was notably absent in the rat. The human, mouse,
and cynomolgus monkey CDA amino acid sequences are similar, but not identical. In vitro
studies evaluating the effect of cedazuridine on mouse or cynomolgus monkey CDA were not
submitted.

The effect of cedazuridine on the proliferation of eight human cancer cell lines, including two
acute leukemia cell lines, was assessed in vitro; cedazuridi ne had no activity on the proliferation
of tumor cells over the concentration range tested (3 to 10 µM) in the cell lines tested.
The effect of cedazuridine on the antitumor activity of decitabine was assessed in two

39
Version date: June 11, 2019 (A ll NOA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID 4636063

NOA Multi-disciplinary Review and Eva luation
NOA 212576, Origina l
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
disseminated leukemia mouse models. In the L1210 disseminated tumor model C02-Fl mice
were inoculated with L1210 mouse ALL cells by IV injection. Three days after inoculation mice
were treated orally with cedazuridine (1-100 mg/kg QOxS); 30-40 minutes later the same mice
were treated orally with decitabine (0.4 mg/kg QOxS). In the KG-1 disseminated tumor model
NSG mice were inoculated with KG-1 human AML cells by IV injection. Twenty-six days after
inoculation mice were treated orally with cedazuridine (1-100 mg/kg QOxS); 30-40 minutes
later the same mice were treated orally with decitabine {0.3 mg/kg QOxS). In the KG-1 model
mice received two cycles of therapy separated by a 23-day non-dosing period. There were
control arms for oral cedazuridine monotherapy, oral decitabine monotherapy, and IV
decitabine monotherapy in both models. Antitumor activities of cedazuridine and/or decitabine
were assessed by median survival time and the percentage increase in life span.
In the L1210 disseminated tumor model, oral cedazuridine treatment administered with a fixed
oral dose of decitabine {0.4 mg/kg) resulted in a dose-dependent increase in life span {41-88%,
p:5:0.0002), relative to vehicle control (see Figure 1). Intravenous decitabine {0.4 mg/kg)
monotherapy resulted in a 47% increase in life span (p<0.0001) relative to vehicle control.
Neither oral cedazuridine nor oral decitabine monotherapy resulted in a statistically significant
increase in life span relative to vehicle control. The increased life span with the cedazuridine
(100 mg/kg) and oral decitabine (0.4 mg/kg) combination relative to IV decitabine {0.4 mg/kg)
monotherapy was statistically significant (p<0.0291) and may be attributed to hepatic
metabolism of IV administered decitabine.

62%, p<0.0001), relative to vehicle control (see Figure 2). Oral and IV decitabine (0.3 mg/kg)
monotherapy resulted in a 9% (p=0.0142) and 31% (p<0.0001) increase in life span,

Safety Pharmacology

A. Cardiovascular

41
Version date: June 11, 2019 (All NOA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Safety pharmacology studies were conducted with cedazuridine to determine its in vitro effects
on the human ether à-go-go related gene (hERG) potassium channel current in stably
transfected human embryonic kidney (HEK293) cells (GLP Study/Report No. 100422.FJT) and on
action potential parameters in isolated guinea pig papillary muscles (Report No. ER-849727-00).
The cumulative data from these studies indicate that cedazuridine does not have any effect on
the cardiovascular system as evidenced by an in vitro concentration giving half-maximal
inhibition (IC 50 ) value of >300 μM for inhibition of hERG channel current and no effects on any
action potential parameters in isolated papillary muscles from guinea pigs.
The potential in vivo effects of cedazuridine on the cardiovascular system and on respiratory
function were evaluated in a stand-alone GLP-compliant study in conscious, naïve male
cynomolgus monkeys using telemetry (Study/Report No. 20123940). Cedazuridine was orally
administered as a single dose to male cynomolgus monkeys (n=4) at dose levels of 0 20 and
200 mg/kg/day on Days 1, 3 and 10, respectively. Cedazuridine was well tolerated and had no
effects on any cardiovascular parameters up to a single oral dose of 200 mg/kg [the
no-observed-effect level (NOEL) in this study].
In addition, cardiovascular assessments included in a 7-day GLP toxicity study in rhesus
monkeys (Study/Report No. LFA00080) and a 4-cycle GLP toxicity study in cynomolgus monkeys
(Study/Report No. 20124863) showed no abnormalities in the electrocardiogram (ECG) data
following repeat once-daily oral administrations of cedazuridine at doses of up to
200 mg/kg/day (the highest dose administered) for 7 consecutive days.
B. Respiratory
In a combination cardiovascular function and respiratory assessment study (details discussed
above under “Cardiovascular”), a single oral dose of cedazuridine of up to 200 mg/kg was not
associated with any effects on respiratory rate, tidal volume, or minute volume in conscious
telemetered monkeys (Study/Report No. 20123940).
C. Central Nervous System (CNS)
Stand-alone nonclinical safety pharmacology assessment of ASTX727 (or cedazuridine or
decitabine) on central nervous system (CNS) function has not been conducted. Cedazuridine
and cedazuridine-derived entities do not show a strong tendency to distribute into tissues and,
following oral administration, are primarily distributed into the GI tract and
metabolic/excretory tissues (e.g., kidney or urinary bladder). Cedazuridine-derived entities do
not cross the blood brain barrier (Study No. 17718; Report No. RPT04536).
The Applicant’s Position:

42
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
The results of safety pharmacology studies did not reveal adverse findings of concern regarding
effects of cedazuridine on the cardiovascular system (based on in vitro hERG and actional
potential assay data and on in vivo evaluations in monkeys) or the respiratory system (based on
evaluations in conscious monkeys). Cedazuridine and cedazuridine-derived entities do not show
a strong tendency to distribute into tissues and, following oral administration, are primarily
distributed into the gastrointestinal tract and metabolic/excretory tissues (e.g., kidney or
urinary bladder). CNS safety pharmacology studies were therefore not conducted. Thus, based
on the results from the studies conducted with cedazuridine, no potential safety pharmacology
concerns for ASTX727 attributable to cedazuridine have been identified.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
We agree with the Applicant’s conclusions for the safety pharmacology studies conducted with
cedazuridine. We note that no safety pharmacology studies were conducted with ASTX727 or
decitabine.

ADME/PK
Data:
Absorption
Comparison of Pharmacokinetic Profiles of Decitabine and E7727 after Single Oral
Administration of Two FDC Tablet Forms of ASTX727 using a Crossover Design (Study/Report
No. APL-FFS-PK-20170522-01)
In this cross-over study, male cynomolgus monkeys were administered ASTX727 [100 mg
cedazuridine (E7727) and 35 mg decitabine] as a fixed-dose combination (FDC) Tablet-A
(earlier formulation used in the FDC stage of the Phase 2 clinical trial) or Tablet-B (new
formulation, developed for the Phase 3 clinical trial). After a 6-day washout period, the
ASTX727 formulation was reversed to complete the crossover. The results of this study
indicate that both forms of FDC tablets showed similar PK profiles and exposures for
decitabine and cedazuridine. Exposures for both decitabine and cedazuridine were higher on
the second day of dosing compared to the first day on each week (i.e., Day 2 vs. Day 1 and
Day 9 vs. Day 8). This effect was more pronounced for decitabine than for cedazuridine.

Distribution
Mass Balance, Radio-Pharmacokinetics in Male and Female CD-1 Mice and Tissue
Distribution by Quantitative Whole Body Autoradiography in Female CD-1 and
C57BL/6 Mice Following a Single Oral Administration of [14C]E7727
(Study No. 17718; Report No. RPT04536)
In a quantitative whole-body autoradiography (QWBA) and mass balance study in female
C57BL/6 and CD-1 mice, a single oral dose of 300 mg/kg (100 μCi/kg) [14C]-cedazuridine
(E7727) was administered and the tissue distribution was profiled for up to 504 hours postdose. Overall, cedazuridine did not show a strong tendency to distribute into tissues. The
metabolic/excretory tissues (kidney, urinary bladder) as well as the tissues of the GI tract
contained the highest distribution of [14C]-cedazuridine derived radioactivity. The tissues with
lowest relative level of distributions in both strains of mice were brain, spinal cord, eye lens,
and white fat, which were all below the quantifiable limit at all sampled time points. Based
on a lack of distribution to the brain and spinal cord, it can be assumed that cedazuridine­
related drug materials do not pass through the blood brain barrier. In addition, no significant
distribution was noted in melanin-containing tissues such as pigmented skin and uveal tract,
suggesting that [14C]-cedazuridine derived radioactivity was not preferentially associating
with melanin.

Metabolism
Based on the metabolite profile of cedazuridine in mice, monkeys, and humans, unchanged
cedazuridine is the most abundant circulating entity followed by its epimer which is the most
prominent circulating metabolite. Toxicological coverage for metabolites is available as all
metabolites detected in vivo in humans were also present in mouse and monkey plasma.

(100 μCi/kg) of [14C]-cedazuridine (E7727).

Excretion
Mass Balance, Radio-Pharmacokinetics in Male and Female CD-1 Mice and Tissue Distribution

The excretion profile of cedazuridine in mice and monkeys indicates that the majority of the
administrated oral dose is not absorbed and is excreted in the feces based on approximately
70% to 77% of the [14C]-cedazuridine dose recovered in feces over a 168-hour post-dose
period in mass balance studies.

The Applicant’s Position:
The PK/TK profile of ASTX727 components cedazuridine and decitabine has been well
characterized in various in vitro and in vivo nonclinical studies. There were no unexpected
findings and the data from these studies are consistent with the pharmacological action of
cedazuridine acting as an inhibitor of CDA as shown by significantly increased systemic
exposures to decitabine following concomitant oral dosing of cedazuridine and decitabine at
clinically relevant doses. Thus, the nonclinical PK/TK data support the clinical administration of
ASTX727 on a schedule that involves daily administration for 5 days per dosing cycle (i.e., Days
1 to 5 of each 28-day cycle).
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
The Applicant stated the nonclinical PK/TK data supported the ASTX727 clinical dosing
schedule. The nonclinical PK/TK data did not directly contribute to the clinical efficacy or AUC
equivalence data that supported the approval of NDA 212576. We have additional comments
on the studies reviewed above and have reviewed additional ADME/PK data.
Absorption
In the comparative PK study evaluating FDC tablets of decitabine and cedazuridine in
cynomolgus monkeys the t 1/2 of cedazuridine was 4-5 hours. The higher exposures (AUC 0-t and
C max ) of decitabine following the second day of dosing is consistent with the expected
pharmacology of the FDC tablets of decitabine and cedazuridine.
The effect of oral cedazuridine followed by time-staggered oral decitabine on the PK profile of
decitabine was evaluated in cynomolgus monkeys. Monkeys were administered a fixed oral
dose of 1 mg/kg cedazuridine concomitantly with a fixed oral dose of 3 mg/kg decitabine, or
following a 30-minute to 2-hour time-delay. Coadministration with cedazuridine resulted in
higher plasma exposures (6.4-fold increase in AUC 0-t , 3.9-fold increase in C max ) of decitabine,
relative to oral decitabine monotherapy. The plasma decitabine exposure increased further
50
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
with time delay; when administered 2 hours after cedazuridine, plasma decitabine AUC 0-t and
C max increased 17.0-fold and 13.2-fold relative to oral decitabine monotherapy, respectively.
Metabolism
The metabolite profile of cedazuridine in cynomolgus monkeys was evaluated as part of a mass
balance and PK study. In this study, male and female cynomolgus monkeys were administered
an oral dose of 20 mg/kg of [14C] cedazuridine; following a 2-week washout period the same
monkeys were administered an IV dose of 2 mg/kg of [14C] cedazuridine. The overall metabolite
profiles were similar for males and females following oral or IV administration. All significant
human metabolites of cedazuridine were detected in mice and/or monkeys.
Excretion
The excretion profile of cedazuridine in cynomolgus monkeys was evaluated as part of the mass
balance and PK study described above. The excretion profile of orally administered
cedazuridine in monkeys was similar to that observed in mice, with the majority of the
administered dose excreted in feces. The excretion profile of IV administered cedazuridine in
monkeys was primarily urinary. It is unclear if the high fecal excretion of cedazuridine after oral
administration could be attributed to unabsorbed drug.
TK data from general toxicology studies
There were no sex-related differences in TK parameters for cedazuridine or decitabine. There
was also little to no accumulation of cedazuridine after once daily administration, which was
the same as for the clinical regimen.

Toxicology
General Toxicology
Data:
Key toxicology findings from the following pivotal studies are summarized below:
• Cedazuridine (E7727): a 4-cycle GLP toxicology study in mice and 4-cycle GLP toxicology
study in monkeys
• Decitabine: 4-cycle GLP toxicology study in rats
• Cedazuridine with decitabine (ASTX727): a 1-cycle (5 days of dosing) PK/PD study in
monkeys

findings.

Other evaluations

Bone marrow cytology findings: Mild morphologic changes in the
erythroid lineage for individual animals in LD and MD groups and
generally marked morphologic changes in the myeloid and erythroid
lineages in the HD group that indicated abnormal cell maturation and
were associated with correlating histopathology. Mildly ↑ numbers of
metarubricytes with lobulated or mildly lobulated nuclei for individual
animals in the LD and MD groups, rarely accompanied by ↑ cell size.
Bone marrow changes in the HD group consisted of the following:
• In the myeloid lineage: Variable proportions of giant band
neutrophils, segmented neutrophils, eosinophils, and basophils (in
addition to earlier stage myeloid progenitors for some animals),
frequently with abnormal nuclear morphology; and
• In the erythroid lineage: Generally, marked ↑ in the numbers of
late stage erythroid progenitors (rubricytes and metarubricytes)
with abnormal nuclear morphology consisting of variably sized
(often large) lobulated, irregularly shaped, and/or fragmented
nuclei. Erythroid lineage cells frequently exhibited asynchronous
maturation, generally reflected by ↑ cytoplasmic volume and
hemoglobinization relative to the maturational stage of the
nucleus. These changes correlated with histopathology findings in
the bone marrow of minimal to marked atypia in one or more
hematopoietic lineages.
All bone marrow cytology changes reversible following the 21-day
recovery period.

HCT = hematocrit; HD = high dose; LD = low dose; M:E =myeloid:erythroid; MD = mid dose; RBC = red blood cell;
WBC = white blood cells

The Applicant’s Position:
In summary, the main repeat-dose toxicity findings from studies conducted in mice, rats, and
monkeys were characterized by reversible myelosuppression (leukopenia, neutropenia,
lymphopenia, and/or decreased platelet counts) due to decitabine, or both (ASTX727),
reversible reproductive tract effects due to cedazuridine, and thymic lymphoid depletion
(cedazuridine, reversible) and testicular effects (partially reversible) due to decitabine.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
We agree with the Applicant’s overall conclusions for the 13-week studies of cedazuridine in
mice and cynomolgus monkeys. There were similar cedazuridine-related hematology findings
observed in both mice and monkeys, although in mice the magnitude of the findings was not
adverse and not included in the Applicant’s review. In both species, the hematology findings
correlated with histopathologic findings in the spleen and/or bone marrow. The Applicant’s
review also captured histopathology findings in the GI tract of monkeys but did not describe
similar findings in mice. Findings in the GI tract of monkeys were generally described as atypia
(including increased single cell death and increased mitoses) while findings in mice were
described as hyperplasia and not adverse. Hyperplasia in mice may have been indicative of a
regenerative response subsequent to necrosis, and thus the etiology of the findings in mice and
monkeys may be similar. The lack of ECG findings in the 13-week monkey study were consistent
with the absence of findings in the stand-alone safety pharmacology study in cynomolgus
monkeys.
The Applicant evaluated the toxicology of orally administered decitabine in the rat. The oral
bioavailability of decitabine is relatively high in rats, which is likely attributed to low CDA
activity in this species. We agree the rat is a relevant species to evaluate the toxicology of orally
administered decitabine. We agree with the Applicant’s overall conclusions for the 13-week
study of oral decitabine in rats; the results were similar to those after IV administered
decitabine, which was reviewed to support the approval of decitabine for injection (NDA
21790).

(pKM101) both in the presence and absence of external metabolic activation.

61
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
GLP compliance: Yes
Test system: Salmonella typhimurium strains TA98, TA100, TA1535, and TA1537 and E. coli
strain WP2 uvrA (pKM101) were incubated with cedazuridine both in the presence and absence
of S9 at the following concentrations: 0 (control), 313, 625, 1250, 2500, or 5000 µg/plate (initial
test) and 0 (control), 1250, 1768, 2500, 3536, or 5000 μg/plate (confirmatory test).
The Applicant’s Position:
It is worth noting that the standard OECD-compliant Ames assay recommends the use of
S. typhimurium strains TA100 and TA1535 (OECD 1997a). While these strains both contain the
same base pair mutation (adenine-thymine to guanine-cytosine), they differ only in that TA100
contains a pKM101 plasmid which is reported to make it more sensitive than TA1535 in
detecting bacterial mutagens (Prival and Zeiger 1998). According to Prival and Zeiger (1998),
analysis of the data on the 36 chemical substances that were judged to be mutagenic in TA1535
and non-mutagenic or equivocal in TA100 indicates that the absolute increases in revertant
colony counts in TA1535 were so small that they could have been missed if the same increases
had occurred in a strain with a higher spontaneous background revertant colony count
(i.e., TA100). The findings from the Ames assay with cedazuridine reflect the analysis from
Prival and Zeiger (1998) in that the increases in the revertant colony counts in TA1535 noted in
both the initial and confirmatory tests were only just doubled at 5,000 µg/plate compared to
colony counts in TA1535 controls and that such increases were absent in the more sensitive
TA100 (Intertek 2018). Thus, the positive findings that were noted only at the highest tested
concentration in strain TA1535 are difficult to interpret given the absence of response in the
more sensitive strain TA100.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
Cedazuridine (5000 μg/plate) was mutagenic in S. typhimurium strain TA1535 in the presence of
external metabolic activation (2.3-fold increase in mean revertants). In the absence of external
metabolic activation (data not shown) cedazuridine (5000 μg/plate) was mutagenic in strain
TA1535 in the initial test (2.2-fold increase in mean revertants), but in the confirmatory test the
1.5-fold increase in mean revertants did not meet the prespecified evaluation criteria (≥2 times
the concurrent vehicle control).

The Applicant’s Position:
The positive genotoxicity findings in the human lymphocytes were generally only observed in
the presence of significant cell toxicity (as indicated by a decrease in the relative mitotic index
compared to concurrent controls) at the highest tested concentrations. All other test article
concentrations in all test conditions caused lesser degrees of cytotoxicity (as indicated by
relative mitotic indices of ≥77%) and, with one exception, did not cause increases in the
percentage of aberrant cells. Given that the positive genotoxicity results in this study were
noted mainly at test article concentrations that caused cytotoxicity levels in excess of the
current OECD-recommended maximum of 55 ± 5% of the concurrent negative controls (OECD
2016), according to the OECD guideline, care should be taken in interpreting the positive
results.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
Cedazuridine was genotoxic in the in vitro chromosome aberration assay under the conditions
tested. There was a concentration-dependent increase in percent aberrant cells and in the 4­
hour treatment with activation, the statistical increase in the aberrant cells occurred at a
cedazuridine concentration that did not cause significant cell toxicity (<55% of concurrent
vehicle control).

Carcinogenicity

Based on its positive genotoxicity, decitabine may be expected to have carcinogenic potential.

Per ICH S9, we agree that carcinogenicity studies are not needed to support the approval of

NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)

6 Clinical Pharmacology

Executive Summary
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
The Applicant seeks approval of INQOVI (ASTX727: decitabine and cedazuridine) for the
treatment of adult patients with myelodysplastic syndromes (MDS) including previously treated
and untreated, de novo and secondary MDS with the following French-American-British
subtypes (refractory anemia, refractory anemia with ringed sideroblasts, refractory anemia
with excess blasts, and chronic myelomonocytic leukemia) and intermediate-1, intermediate-2,
and high-risk one International Prognostic Scoring System groups. The proposed INQOVI dosing
regimen is one tablet (35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine) taken orally once daily, on an
empty stomach, on first five consecutive days of each 28-day cycle. Patients should avoid food 2
hours before and 2 hours after each INQOVI dose.
The Clinical Pharmacology section of the NDA is supported by the evaluation and analysis of
comparative pharmacokinetics (PK) and pharmacodynamics (PD) of INQOVI versus IV
decitabine, multiple dose PK of decitabine and cedazuridine, and exposure-response
relationships for efficacy and safety.
Based on the efficacy and safety results in the Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies in patients with MDS
and the comparative PK and PD assessment of INQOVI and IV decitabine in the Phase 3 study,
the proposed INQOVI dosing regimen of 35 mg decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine as a single
tablet administered orally once daily for Days 1-5 of each 28-day cycle with the dose
modification schema is acceptable. The geometric mean ratio (90% CI) for the 5 day cumulative
decitabine exposure between INQOVI and IV decitabine was 0.99 ( 0.93, 1.06). The high 5-day
cumulative decitabine exposure was associated with a higher likelihood of adverse reactions,
including any grade neutropenia and thrombocytopenia, that were mostly managed by dose
delays and dose reductions.
Analysis of the data provided by the Applicant showed that an increase in cumulative
decitabine exposure with decrease in body surface area or body weight was not clinically
meaningful. The cumulative decitabine exposures between INQOVI and IV decitabine were
comparable for patients with mild to moderate renal impairment, mild hepatic impairment, and
in the presence or absence of proton pump inhibitors. No dose adjustment is recommended for
patients based on body size or in patients with mild to moderate renal impairment, mild
hepatic impairment, and concomitant use of proton pump inhibitors. The results of the foodeffect study were found to be unreliable, and the QT-concentration assessment did not
adequately characterize the effect of cedazuridine on QT/QTc interval prolongation and post
marketing clinical studies will be needed to address these issues.

tablet orally once daily for the first 5 consecutive days of each 28-day cycle in patients with
MDS with the dose modification schema in the event of adverse reactions is acceptable. From a
Clinical Pharmacology standpoint, the NDA is approvable provided the Applicant and the FDA
reach an agreement regarding the labeling language.

Summary of Clinical Pharmacology Assessment
Pharmacology and Clinical Pharmacokinetics
Data:
Mechanism of Action:
ASTX727, a combination of cedazuridine (new molecular entity) and decitabine, is an oral
hypomethylating agent (HMA).
Decitabine is a nucleoside metabolic inhibitor that is believed to exert its antineoplastic effects
after phosphorylation and direct incorporation into DNA and inhibition of DNA
methyltransferase, causing hypomethylation of DNA and cellular differentiation and/or
apoptosis. When administered orally, decitabine is degraded by cytidine deaminase (CDA) and
has limited oral bioavailability.
Cedazuridine (E7727) is a competitive inhibitor of CDA with a concentration giving half-maximal
inhibition (IC 50 ) value of 281 ± 58 nmol/L. The potency of the E7727-epimer to inhibit CDA is
only 1/10th of E7727.
Oral administration of cedazuridine with decitabine enhances the oral bioavailability of
decitabine via inhibition of first pass metabolism of decitabine in the gut and liver by CDA.
Pharmacodynamics:
•
•

Administration of ASTX727 transiently increased plasma concentrations of cytidine as
expected for the CDA inhibitor, cedazuridine.
Administration of ASTX727 resulted in a similar reduction in long interspersed nucleotide
element-1 (LINE-1) DNA methylation in peripheral blood mononuclear cells to that of
69
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)

•

administration of IV decitabine 20 mg/m2. At the recommended ASTX727 dose, a maximal
of near maximal pharmacodynamic effect of LINE-1 demethylation was observed over the
range of decitabine systemic exposures, based on PK-PD modeling.
The potential for QT prolongation effect of ASTX727 was evaluated in the Phase 3 study by
performing limited electrocardiogram (ECG) assessment before and after dosing at the
expected t max of both decitabine and cedazuridine. No large mean changes (i.e., >10 msec)
in the heart rate-corrected QT interval (QTc) interval were observed. An exposure-QTc
analysis suggested no concentration-dependent QTc interval prolongation for either
decitabine or cedazuridine.

General Pharmacokinetics
•
•
•
•

Cedazuridine exposures were dose proportional across the dose range of 40 to 100 mg.
Decitabine 5-day AUC 0-t over the dosing range of 20 to 40 mg was dose proportional.
Both cedazuridine and decitabine reach steady-state on Day 2 of administration of ASTX727,
with low accumulation ratios (1.3 to 1.6 for cedazuridine and 1.7 to 1.8 for decitabine).
Steady-state concentrations of decitabine were reached on Day 1 of dosing with IV
decitabine 20 mg/m2.

AUC equivalence:
The Phase 3 study showed that the extent of absorption (total cycle 5-day AUC 0-24 ) of
decitabine from oral dosing with ASTX727 was equivalent to IV administration of decitabine
(Table 4).

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Absorption:
•

•
•

•

Following oral administration of ASTX727, cedazuridine was rapidly absorbed and the
median time to maximum plasma concentration (t max ) was 3 hours (range, 0.52 to
7.9 hours). Approximately 47.5% of an oral dose of cedazuridine was absorbed and the oral
bioavailability of cedazuridine was 20.7%.
Following oral administration of ASTX727, decitabine was rapidly absorbed and t max was
1 hour (range, 0.3 to 3). The t max with IV administration was at the end of the infusion.
When coadministered, cedazuridine (40, 60, or 100 mg) increased the oral bioavailability of
decitabine (20, 30, or 40 mg) compared to that of oral decitabine alone by (R value) 3.68­
12.0-fold.
While administration of ASTX727 with a high-fat, high-calorie meal (approximately 800-1000
calories, with approximately 150 calories from protein, approximately 250 calories from
carbohydrates, and approximately 500-600 calories from fat) had little effect on
cedazuridine and cedazuridine-epimer exposures, decitabine exposure parameters were
approximately 40% (AUC) to 54% (maximum plasma concentration [C max ]) lower compared
to those following administration under fasted conditions. Administration with food
delayed cedazuridine t max slightly but had little effect on systemic exposures.

Distribution
• Cedazuridine was approximately 35% bound to human plasma proteins in vitro. The
geometric mean (coefficient of variation [CV]) of apparent volume of distribution (V/F) for
cedazuridine was 296 L (51%).
• Decitabine was approximately 5% bound to human plasma proteins in vitro. After
administration of ASTX727, the geometric mean (CV) of decitabine V/F at steady state was
417 L (54%).
Elimination (Metabolism and Excretion)
Metabolism:
• The primary metabolic pathway for cedazuridine was conversion to the epimer. There was
also some dehydrogenation and some conjugation with urea.
Elimination:
• Renal clearance was the main route of excretion of orally absorbed cedazuridine and the
cedazuridine-epimer.
• After oral administration of [14C]-cedazuridine, 17.1% (range, 12.0% to 24.1%) of the
administered dose was excreted in the urine as unchanged cedazuridine within 75 hours.
The cedazuridine-epimer excreted in the urine was 17.5% of the orally administered
cedazuridine dose.

71
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
•

•

•
•

•

After IV administration of [14C]-cedazuridine, 80.9% (range, 73.5% to 88.4%) of the total
radioactivity was recovered in the urine and 0.6% was recovered in the feces within
72 hours.
After oral administration of [14C]-cedazuridine, the mean total recovery was 96.9% (range,
93.2% to 100.2%) over the 120-hour collection interval with the majority of radioactivity
recovered in urine and feces in the first 72 hours postdose (92.2%). The renal clearance
(CL r ) was 4.46 L/h (range, 3.01 to 5.47 L/h).
The major elimination pathway for decitabine is metabolic, by CDA, and physico-chemical
degradation at physiological conditions.
Following a single oral dose of ASTX727, the mean (CV) terminal elimination half-life (t 1/2 ) of
cedazuridine was 6.3 hours (18%). The apparent clearance (CL/F) of cedazuridine was
30.6 L/h at Day 1 and 30.3 L/h at steady state.
Following a single oral dose of ASTX727, the mean t 1/2 (CV) of decitabine was 1.2 hours
(23%) compared to 1.0 hours (47%) for IV decitabine. After administration of ASTX727,
decitabine CL/F was 342 L/h at Day 1 and 197 L/h at steady state.

Specific Populations
•

•

Age, sex, body weight, or mild to moderate hepatic impairment (by NCI criteria) did not
have a clinically meaningful effect on the PK of decitabine or cedazuridine after
administration of ASTX727. Mild or moderate renal impairment (NCI criteria) had an effect
on cedazuridine exposures, but based on the relatively small magnitude of the effect this
was considered not clinically meaningful.
The effect of severe hepatic impairment or of severe renal impairment on the PK of
decitabine and cedazuridine is unknown.

Drug-Drug Interactions
•
•
•
•
•
•

The exposure of cedazuridine was unaffected by coadministration with decitabine.
Model-based analyses of proton pump inhibitor (PPI) coadministration showed that PPI had
no effect on the PK of decitabine or cedazuridine.
Cedazuridine is a potent inhibitor of CDA, so it would affect the metabolism and exposure of
any coadministered drug that is metabolized via this enzyme.
In vitro, cedazuridine did not induce CYP1A, CYP2B6, CYP2C9, and CYP3A and did not inhibit
any of the CYP isoforms tested (i.e., CYP1A, CYP2B6, CYP2C8, CYP2C9, CYP2C19, CYP2D6,
CYP2E1, and CYP3A).
Cedazuridine was not an inhibitor of P-gp, BCRP, MATE1, MATE2-K, OAT1, OAT3, OATP1B1,
OATP1B3, OCT2, CNT1, ENT1, or ENT2. A weak inhibition of CNT2 and CNT3 was observed,
but only at the highest concentration tested.
In in vitro studies, cedazuridine was not metabolized by human liver S9 fraction or liver
cytosol and was not a substrate for the majority of transporters. Although cedazuridine was
transported by CNT1, CNT3, and ENT2, the potential for drug-drug interactions with co
medications is low.

Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
•

Decitabine is not a substrate for CYP enzymes, and the plasma protein binding of decitabine
is negligible. Decitabine is unlikely to inhibit or induce CYP enzymes.

Exposure-adverse event analysis:
•

Model-based analyses of exposure-safety relationships using pooled data of Phase 2 part of
Study ASTX727-01 and from Study ASTX727-02 (Phase 3) focused on mainly hematological
parameters such as neutropenia and thrombocytopenia determined that none of the trends
in these analyses were significant, suggesting a similar risk of thrombocytopenia or
neutropenia with oral versus IV decitabine within or across exposure ranges and weight
groups (quartiles) and that no dose adjustment is needed based on the subject’s weight.

The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
Refer to Table 5 in Sections 6.3.1.
General Dosing and Therapeutic Individualization
6.2.2.1.

General Dosing

Data:
The selection of the dose and regimen is based on the decitabine 5-day AUC equivalence of
ASTX727 with IV decitabine (20 mg/m2 Daily×5). Dose levels for the combination to achieve
total cycle AUC that match the IV were confirmed in the Phase 2 study (Table 12 and Table 13)
and the full AUC equivalence was established in the Phase 3 study (Table 6). In addition,
ASTX727 treatment at the recommended dose and regimen achieved almost identical
demethylation to IV decitabine, as measured by %LINE 1 demethylation (Table 8, Table 9, and
Figure 3). Likewise, the safety data from the Phase 2 and Phase 3 studies largely mirrors the
expected safety profile of IV decitabine in the same patient population.
Administration of ASTX727 with food (high-fat, high-calorie meal) had little effect on
cedazuridine and cedazuridine epimer exposures; however, decitabine exposure parameters
were approximately 40% (AUC) to 53.5% (C max ) lower compared to those following
administration under fasted conditions (Table 16).
See Section 6.3.1 for more details.
The Applicant’s Position:
The recommended dose of ASTX727 is 1 tablet containing 100 mg cedazuridine and 35 mg
decitabine taken orally once daily on Days 1 through 5 of each 28-day cycle for a minimum of 4
(b) (4)
cycles. ASTX727 tablets should be taken with water on an empty stomach.

73
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position regarding the proposed dose of one ASTX727 (35 mg
decitabine and 100 mg cedazuridine) tablet once daily for first the 5-days of each 28-day cycle
under fasted conditions, based on the demonstration of comparable PK and PD in Study
ASTS727-02 between ASTX727 and IV decitabine and efficacy in Study ASTX727-01-B and Study
ASTS727-02. FDA agrees with the Applicant’s proposed strategy for dose modification for
hematological toxicity, including the proposed schema for dose reductions.
FDA does not agree with the Applicant’s position regarding the food effect due to inconsistency
in the PK results in Study ASTX727-04 (refer to Section 6.3.2.4). There is inadequate data
evaluating the effect of food on ASTX727. Additional data regarding the effect of food on
ASTX727 is needed as a post-marketing commitment. FDA recommends that patients should
avoid taking food 2 hours before and 2 hours after administration of ASTX727, consistent with
the fasted conditions followed in Studies ASTX727-01-B and ASTX727-02.
6.2.2.2.

Therapeutic Individualization

Data:
Exploratory subgroup analyses by age (32 to 90 years), sex, body weight (41 to 158 kg), or mild
to moderate hepatic impairment (total bilirubin > upper limit of normal [ULN] up to ≤3 × ULN)
did not have a clinically meaningful effect on the PK of decitabine or cedazuridine after
administration of ASTX727. Mild or moderate renal impairment (creatinine clearance [CRCL]
≥20 to 59 mL/min/1.73m2) had an effect on cedazuridine exposures, but this was not
considered to be clinically meaningful. The effects of severe hepatic impairment or severe renal
impairment on the PK of decitabine and cedazuridine is unknown.
Drug-drug interaction studies have not been conducted with cedazuridine or decitabine. In vitro
studies indicate that neither cedazuridine nor decitabine is likely to inhibit or induce major
human cytochrome P450 enzymes. Neither cedazuridine nor decitabine is an inhibitor or a
substrate of major human drug transporters. Cedazuridine is an inhibitor of CDA.
Concomitant PPI medication was assessed as a potential covariate in the population PK (PopPK)
model because of the possibility that such medications could affect the rate of conversion of
cedazuridine into its much less active epimer. The assessment showed, however, that
concomitant PPI medication usage did not have a significant impact on decitabine,
cedazuridine, and cedazuridine-epimer parameters and subsequent exposures, suggesting that
drugs modifying gastric pH are unlikely to affect the PK of cedazuridine or decitabine after
ASTX727 administration.
The Applicant’s Position:
In regards to drug-drug interactions (DDIs), the following are recommended:
74

INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
•
•
•

Drugs metabolized via CYP450 pathways: no modification
Drugs metabolized by CDA: avoided concomitant use
Proton Pump Inhibitors (or other gastric pH modifying agents): no modification needed

In regards to special populations, the following are recommended:
•
•

Renal Impairment: no modification needed for patients with mild to moderate
impairment. No data on patients with severe impairment
(b) (4)
Hepatic Impairment:
No data on patients with severe impairment

The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position of no dose modifications based on age (32 to 90
years), sex, body weight (41 to 158 kg), body surface area, mild to moderate renal impairment
(creatinine clearance ≥30 mL/min), mild hepatic impairment (total bilirubin > ULN to 1.5 × ULN,
or AST>ULN). The FDA agrees with the Applicant’s position that no dose modifications are
necessary for the concomitant use of CYP modulators and that the concomitant use of drugs
that are metabolized by CDA should be avoided. FDA agrees that no dosing modifications are
needed with concomitant use of PPIs or other gastric pH modifying agents.
There was only one patient with severe renal impairment (creatinine clearance 15 to <30
mL/min) in the Phase 2 and 3 studies, and hence there is insufficient data to reliably
demonstrate the effect of severe renal impairment on the PK and safety of ASTX727. Therefore,
additional information regarding the exposure and safety of ASTX727 in patients with severe
renal impairment and end-stage renal disease will be needed as a post-marketing requirement.
We disagree with the Applicant’s position on therapeutic individualization in patients with
moderate hepatic impairment. There were only 3 patients with moderate hepatic impairment
and no patients with severe hepatic impairment in the clinical studies. Hence, there is
insufficient data to reliably demonstrate the effect of moderate and severe hepatic impairment
on the PK and safety of ASTX727. Additional information regarding the exposure and safety of
ASTX727 in patients with moderate and severe hepatic impairment will be needed as a postmarketing requirement.

6.2.2.3.

Outstanding Issues

Data:
The studies included in the PopPK analysis (the Phase 2 parts of Study ASTX727-01 and the
Phase 3 Study ASTX727-02) did not include subjects with severe renal impairment or with
severe hepatic impairment. These studies also did not have sufficient racial diversity to inform
potential correlations with regard to race or ethnicity. ECG evaluations with time-matched PK
75
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
were included in Phase 3 but were limited to single tracing before treatment and another posttreatment at the expected t max of decitabine and cedazuridine on Day 3 of Cycle 1 and 2. This
was due to already demanding PK assessments in the study design to support the primary
endpoint of the trial.
A thorough QT study was not conducted with ASTX727 as these studies are challenging to
conduct in this disease population with advanced age and often cardiac comorbidities and use
(b) (4)
of concomitant medications.

The Applicant’s Position:
Neither race nor ethnicity is expected to affect the clinical pharmacology of ASTX727, but this
has not been verified.
The effects of severe renal impairment or severe hepatic impairment on the clinical
pharmacology of ASTX727 are unknown at this time.
(b) (4)

Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
• FDA disagrees with the Applicant’s assessment of the QT prolongation risk. FDA notes that
the PK and ECG data from ASTX727-02, used in the Applicant’s QT risk assessment, are not
sufficient to adequately characterize the effect of cedazuridine from ASTX727 on the QTc
interval (refer to 6.3.2.5 for details). Evaluation of the QT prolongation potential of
cedazuridine will be required. This will need to be assessed as a post-marketing
requirement.
•

There is insufficient data to assess the exposure and safety of ASTX727 in patients with
moderate or severe hepatic impairment (refer to 6.3.2.3 for details). This will need to be
assessed as a post-marketing requirement.

•

There is insufficient data to assess the exposure and safety of ASTX727 in patients with
severe renal impairment and end-stage renal disease (refer to 6.3.2.3 for details). This will
need to be assessed as a post-marketing requirement.

•

The results in Study ASTX727-04 do not permit for an adequate assessment of the effect of
food on the pharmacokinetics of ASTX-727 (refer to 6.3.2.4 for details). There is no data to
adequately describe and label the effect of food on the pharmacokinetics of ASTX727. This
will need to be evaluated as a post-marketing commitment.

6.3.2.

Clinical Pharmacology Questions
6.3.2.1 Does the cl inical pharmacology program provide su pportive
evidence of effectiveness?

78
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NOA/ BLA r eviews)
Reference ID 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Data:
Yes. Supportive evidence of effectiveness is provided based on PK assessments in the Phase 2
(ASTX727-01-B) and the Phase 3 pivotal study (ASTX727-02) and based on pharmacodynamic
effects in the Phase 2 and 3 studies.
Pharmacology
In vitro, cedazuridine was a potent inhibitor of CDA (IC 50 of 0.281 µM) and was able to increase
the t 1/2 of the CDA-mediated metabolism of gemcitabine from ≤36 minutes to ≥66 hours.
Pharmacokinetic Equivalence
In the Phase 3 Study ASTX727-02, ASTX727 administered Daily ×5 was compared with IV
decitabine 20 mg/m2 Daily ×5. The primary analysis of the primary study endpoint showed that
the 5-day AUC 0-24 percentage ratio of geometric LSM for ASTX727 relative to IV decitabine was
98.93% (90% CI 92.66%, 105.6%). The 2-sided 90% CI was contained entirely within the
prespecified range of 80% to 125% for the primary analysis (Table 6).
Results of the secondary analyses further supported the primary analysis AUC 0-24 (Table 7) in
that all analyses show that 90% CI for the oral/IV ratio are contained entirely between 80% and
125%. The range of decitabine exposures after oral ASTX727 at the recommended doses
(100 mg cedazuridine and 35 mg decitabine) largely overlapped with the range of exposures
after IV decitabine at 20 mg/m2 (Figure 1).
