No specific patient reported outcomes were analyzed in the clinical trials for this application.

Safety Analyses by Demographic Subgroups
Data:
Adverse events were analyzed by subgroups for a variety of intrinsic (age, sex, race, renal
impairment, hepatic impairment, BSA, study disease) and extrinsic factors (country) in the
integrated population.
Age
There was no consistent and clinically notable pattern of increasing AE incidence either overall
or in the different SOCs from between the younger age group (<65 years) to the oldest age
group (≥75 years) particularly in the SOCs of interest such as Blood and Lymphatic System
Disorders and Infections and Infestations (Table 68).
Individual AEs that were notably higher (more than double) in the oldest age group (≥75 years)
compared to the youngest age group (<65 years) included:
• sepsis (12.2% vs 3.8%)
• contusion (14.9% vs 3.8%), which were reported as bruises, some of which may have
been due to multiple venipuncture for PK draws
• blood creatinine increase (12.2% vs 3.8%)
Individual AEs that were notably higher (more than double) in the youngest age group (<65
years) compared to the oldest group (≥75 years) included:
• nausea (48.1% vs 17.6%)
• vomiting (19.2% vs5.4%)
• headache (26.9% vs 10.8%)
• insomnia (13.5% vs 5.4%)
• oropharyngeal pain (15.4% vs 4.1%)

INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Body Surface Area:
In addition, since ASTX727 contains a fixed dose of decitabine (35 mg), it is important to note
that the safety analysis by BSA quartiles showed no consistent trend of higher AEs for ASTX727
in subjects in the smallest BSA quartile compared with BSA-based dosing of IV decitabine. While
subjects in the smallest BSA quartile had a higher incidence of certain AEs with ASTX727 in
Cycles 1 or 2, the results were similar to IV decitabine, indicating that the higher AE incidence is
probably related to other intrinsic baseline patients characteristics factors in that cohort
compared with subjects in the larger BSA quartiles. Subjects in the smallest BSA quartile had
lower baseline neutrophils and a higher incidence of transfusion dependence at baseline.
Other
There was no consistent clinically notable pattern of a higher incidence of AEs in one race group
versus the other (White vs non-White).
There were no notable safety differences in subjects with mild to moderate renal or hepatic
impairment. Anemia and febrile neutropenia tended to have similar or lower incidence in the
moderately impaired versus normal subjects while neutropenia, leukopenia, and
thrombocytopenia tended to be higher in the moderately impaired compared to normal
subjects. No clinically notable increase in AEs in other SOCs was observed in hepatically
impaired subjects compared to normal subjects. The interpretation is limited due to the small
sample size of hepatically impaired subjects (N=22) and the fact that most of them had only
mild impairment. There was also no notable difference in AEs between MDS and CMML
patients; however, there was a general trend toward lower incidence of AEs in CMML subjects
compared to MDS subjects in most SOCs.
The Applicant’s Position:
The subgroup analyses of safety data did not show marked or notable differences in the safety
profile of ASTX727 for any individual subgroup.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment.
Specific Safety Studies/Clinical Trials
Data:
No specific studies were conducted with ASTX727 to evaluate a specific safety concern.
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable

Neoplasms benign, malignant and unspecified (incl cysts and polyps) SOC. This included 2 (1%)
with squamous cell carcinoma, 2 (1%) with squamous cell carcinoma of skin, 2 (1%) with basal
cell carcinoma, 2 (1%) with seborrheic keratosis, and one each of adenocarcinoma gastric,
hypergammaglobulinaemia benign monoclonal, melanocytic naevus, pancreatic carcinoma
metastatic, refractory cytopenia with unilineage dysplasia, small cell lung cancer, and squamous
cell carcinoma of the tongue. The median time to first event was 148.5 days (range 12-1285)
days. Given the short median time to onset, these malignancies are less likely to be related to
treatment with ASTX727.
Excluding the cases of seborrheic keratosis, hypergammaglobulinaemia benign monoclonal,
melanocytic naevus, and refractory cytopenia with unilineage dysplasia (the disease under
study), the incidence of second malignancies was 10 (5%). Two patients with second
malignancies of basal and squamous cell carcinoma had a prior history of skin cancer (basal
carcinoma) and had relatively short drug exposure at the time of the event (Cycle 4 and Cycle
7). Another 4 patients had their second malignancy after < 6 cycles of decitabine (Cycles 2, 3, 4,
and 5). Therefore, the second malignancies observed are most likely unrelated to ASTX727.
Human Reproduction and Pregnancy
Data:
No pregnancies were reported in the clinical trials.
Based on its mechanism of action and findings in animals, decitabine (one of the active
ingredients in the ASTX727 FDC tablet) can cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant
woman. Decitabine alters DNA synthesis and is expected to result in adverse reproductive
effects. In nonclinical studies with decitabine in mice and rats, decitabine was teratogenic,
fetotoxic, and embryotoxic (Dacogen USPI 2019).
Due to the known adverse reproductive effects of decitabine, no reproductive or
developmental toxicity studies have been conducted with ASTX727 or cedazuridine alone.
Pregnant and breast-feeding women were excluded from enrollment in ASTX727 clinical
studies. ASTX727 or its components (cedazuridine and decitabine) have not been administered
orally to women who were pregnant or breast-feeding in the ASTX727 clinical program and no
information is available on the use of ASTX727 in pregnant women.
There are no data on the effects of oral administration of cedazuridine or decitabine or its
metabolites in human milk, the effects on the breastfed child, or on milk production.
The Applicant’s Position:
Based on findings from human data, animal studies, and the mechanism of action for
decitabine, ASTX727 may cause fetal harm when administered to a pregnant woman.

Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)

INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Because many drugs are excreted in human milk, and because of the potential for serious
adverse reactions from ASTX727 in a nursing child, advise lactating women to avoid
breastfeeding during treatment with ASTX727 and for at least 1 week after the last dose.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
Lactating women are advised to avoid breastfeeding during treatment with ASTX727 and for at
least 2 weeks after the last dose, consistent with the recommendation for IV decitabine.

Data:
ASTX727 has not been investigated in pediatric patients.
The Applicant’s Position:
Not applicable
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA confirms the Applicant’s assessment.
Overdose, Drug Abuse Potential, Withdrawal, and Rebound
Data:
There were no reports of overdose.
There is no known antidote for overdosage with ASTX727 FDC tablets. Higher doses of
decitabine may be associated with increased myelosuppression, including prolonged
neutropenia and thrombocytopenia.
There is no evidence that ASTX727 is habit forming or could lead to dependence.
ASTX727 can be stopped abruptly with no withdrawal effects. Potential clinical benefit may be
lost if ASTX727 is stopped too early. Once treatment is stopped, blood levels of decitabine,
cedazuridine, and cedazuridine-epimer should be non-detectable within 24, 72, and 72 hours,
respectively.
The Applicant’s Position:
Overdose of ASTX727 should be managed with supportive care of expected adverse events of
myelosuppression and infection.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA confirms the Applicant’s assessment.

Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
Data:
ASTX727 FDC tablets are not currently marketed in any country.
The Applicant’s Position:
Postmarketing data from IV decitabine are included in ASTX727 proposed labelling.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
The Applicant added differentiation syndrome (DS) and interstitial lung disease (ILD) to the
post-marketing experience section of the proposed drug label. DS is in the USPI for IV
decitabine, but ILD is not. The Sponsor responded to a labeling query on 5/13/2020 indicating
that ILD is included in the Canadian PM and the EMA SmPC of Dacogen. However, given that ILD
was not in the USPI for IV decitabine, the IV decitabine label was updated via a labeling
supplement to include the risk of ILD. Both DS and ILD are now included in the post-marketing
experience section. Sweet’s syndrome (acute febrile neutrophilic dermatosis), which is related
to DS, is listed in the post-marketing section for IV decitabine, but Sweet’s syndrome occurred
in ASTX727 clinical trials (1%), so was included in Section 6.1.
Expectations on Safety in the Postmarket Setting
Data:
The safety profile of decitabine from the approved indication and long-term use in clinical
setting both in MDS and off label in other myeloid malignancies have been extensively studied
and reported in the literature together with substantial postmarketing experience globally. It is
unlikely that ASTX727 use will result in additional safety concerns than what is already known
for decitabine, considering the equivalent systemic exposure to IV decitabine and long-term
safety assessment in the application (up to 29 cycles in Phase 2 and up to 35 cycles in Phase 1).
The Applicant’s Position:
Use of ASTX727 is not expected to result in new safety concerns that were not already
described for IV decitabine and ASTX727 NDA submission.
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment but notes that it is unknown whether cedazuridine
and/or the oral route of administration may contribute to certain adverse effects of ASTX727
that may yet be unknown.
Integrated Assessment of Safety
Data:
277
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Decitabine in ASTX727 has known risks of cytotoxicity, mainly myelosuppression (hematologic
toxicity), and infection consequences due to neutropenia; however, these complications are
also common in the disease population. In addition, ASTX727 has a fixed dose of decitabine as
opposed to IV decitabine, which is dosed based on BSA. ASTX727 also contains an NCE, the CDA
inhibitor cedazuridine, and is dosed orally. This could result in added risks to those already
known for IV decitabine, which are discussed below:
•

•

•

•

As expected, ASTX727 was associated with common AEs of thrombocytopenia, neutropenia,
anemia, and febrile neutropenia. The overall incidence of these AEs (all grades and Grade
≥3 AEs) was generally similar between ASTX727 and IV decitabine in Cycle 1 or 2. Infection
SAEs of pneumonia and sepsis were less common in both treatments, with pneumonia
occurring at a similar incidence in the ASTX727 and IV decitabine cycles but with sepsis
reported at a higher incidence for ASTX727. The slightly higher incidence of SAEs of sepsis
may have been more related to baseline characteristics in Cycle 1 and 2 since the incidence
was generally lower with ASTX727 in Cycles ≥3 with continued treatment where patients
may have been stabilized by treatment response. There was some imbalance in baseline
disease characteristics between Sequence A (ASTX727 in Cycle 1) and B (IV decitabine in
Cycle 1) in Phase 3. The risks of cytopenias and associated infections/bleeding are the same
risks as the target disease of MDS and CMML (Steensma 2018; Parikh and Tefferi 2012).
These complications are easily monitored with blood counts and manageable with the use
of appropriate standard of care therapies (e.g., growth factors and anti-infectives) by
physicians experienced in the treatment of this disease. ASTX727 treatment can also be
delayed and/or dose reduced to manage these complications.
Analyses of safety data by BSA quartiles did not show any notable safety profile differences
between quartiles that was not also present for IV decitabine. In addition, analyses of
exposure-safety for certain hematological AEs showed that incidence of the selected AEs
were similar across the full range of decitabine exposures achieved with the fixed dose
treatment with ASTX727.
Analysis of GI disorders SOC showed no differences in the overall incidence of GI AEs
between ASTX727 and IV decitabine. Notably, incidence of individual GI AEs of Grade ≥3
was <2% for ASTX727 and IV decitabine. No additional risk from the oral administration of
ASTX727 was identified.
Overview of the safety profile of ASTX727 did not identify a safety risk associated with
cedazuridine. The relatively short and intermittent (5 days per month) CDA inhibition only
had the desired effect of enabling oral delivery of decitabine with no other safety
consequences apparent from the safety data. However, other nucleosides that are also
metabolized by CDA should not be co-administered on the same day as cedazuridine.

The Applicant' s Position:
IV decitabine is already approved for the treatment of MOS and CMML with a known positive
benefit-risk profile. Based on the above data, ASTX727 is expected to provide similar benefits to
those achieved with IV decitabine. ASTX727 treatment involves simi lar risks to those known for
IV decitabine. There was no notable added risk observed as a resu lt of cedazuridine treatment
or the ora l administration of ASTX727. The decitabine exposures from ASTX727 fixed-dose
administration were largely overlapping with those of IV decitabine in individual patients). The
fixed decitabine dose in ASTX727 did not result in added risk particularly for patients with
small er body weight/ body size . The range of decitabine exposures across the different body
weights were also largely overlapping (Figure 8) and exposure-safety ana lyses showed simi lar
AE incidence across the range of decitabine exposures). The data support the conclusion that
ASTX727 (cedazuridine 100 mg and decitabine 35 mg; Dailyx5) is associated with a simi lar
positive benefit-risk to that of the approved IV decitabine dose and regimen of 20 mg/ m 2
Dai lyx5 in patients with intermediate or high-risk M OS or CMML.
The Regulatory Authorities' Assessment:
FDA performed an updated analysis of the overall safety profile of ASTX727 based on data from
the 120-day safety update. The on-treatment fatal TEARs listed are as per FDA adjud ication.

Overall, the poo led SAS demonstrated the already known safety profile of IV decitabine. The
study populatio n was monito red for deaths, SAEs, co mmo n AEs, and common laboratory tests.
A total of 16 (8%) of patients treated with ASTX727 died during or within 30 days after study
treatment. Considering only the first cycle of therapy, on-treatment deaths were similarly low
(1-2%) in patients treated with ASTX727 or IV decitabine. The most frequent cause of death was
primary disease. Treatment-related fata l ARs included sepsis, septic shock, pneumonia,
respiratory failure, cerebral hemorrhage, and sudden death. Treatment-related fatal ARs in the
first cycle were due to septic shock, pneumonia, and respiratory fai lure in patients treated with
ASTX727 and IV decitabine.
ASTX727 was generally well-to lerated. The most common TEARs ( in ci dence~ 15% and ~ 2%
more frequent with ASTX727 compared to IV decitabine) during Cycle 1 were
thro mbocytopenia, fatigue, nausea, dyspnea, diarrhea, dizziness, headache, and leukopenia.
The most frequent Grade ~ 3 TEARs (incidence ~ 5% and ~ 2% more frequent with ASTX727
compared to IV decitabine) were thrombocytopenia, neutropenia, dyspnea, leukopenia, and
sepsis. The most frequent serious AE during the first cycle was febrile neutropenia, which
occurred at a lower rate with ASTX727 {8%) compared t o IV decitabine (12%). However, the SAR
of sepsis was more common with ASTX727 (6%) compared to IV decitabine (2%) during Cycle 1.

281
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NOA/ BLA r eviews)
Reference ID 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
The most common TEAR during all cycles of ASTX727 was thrombocytopenia, with 62%
incidence of all-grade and 54% incidence of Grade 3 or higher thrombocytopenia. Other ARs
occurring in ≥ 20% of patients were neutropenia, fatigue, anemia, arthralgias, constipation,
myalgias, mucositis, nausea, dyspnea, diarrhea, rash, febrile neutropenia, dizziness, edema,
cough, headache, leukopenia, decreased appetite, URI, pneumonia, and transaminase
increased. Additional Grade ≥ 3 TEARs (≥ 10%) included neutropenia, anemia, febrile
neutropenia, leukopenia, pneumonia, and sepsis.
Discontinuation due to AR occurred in only 5% of patients in the pooled SAS. The most frequent
ARs leading to discontinuation were febrile neutropenia (1%) and pneumonia (1%).
The important risks of myelosuppression and embryo-fetal toxicity are unchanged from IV
decitabine based on the data presented in the submission.
Overall, given the historically poor outcomes of patients with MDS who are candidates for
hypomethylating agent therapy, the level of toxicity observed with ASTX727 is acceptable for
the clinical benefit observed. The toxicity profile is very similar to that observed with IV
decitabine, with perhaps more myelosuppression, which can be managed with close monitoring
and dose interruption or reduction, if required.

SUMMARY AND CONCLUSIONS
Statistical Issues
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
The submitted clinical data from studies ASTX727-01-B (phase 2) and ASTX727-02 (phase 3)
demonstrated:
1) a CR rate of 18-21% with median duration of CR responses of 7.5 – 8.7 months. These
results are similar to the ORRs (CR+PR) reported in the USPI for IV decitabine, which
were 17% for the randomized trial and 16% for the single arm trial.
2) 49-53% of patients dependent on transfusions at baseline achieved at least 56 days
transfusion independence post-baseline and 63-64% of patients transfusion
independent at baseline were able to maintain that transfusion independence for at
least 56 days post-baseline.
Of note,
1) both studies ASTX727-01-B (phase 2) and ASTX727-02 (phase 3) are considered as single
arm trial in terms of efficacy assessment, therefore results of time to event endpoints
are not interpretable.

the Applicant. The review team recommends approval of the NOA.

target disease in the pediatric population.

FDA agrees with the Applicant’s assessment. Pediatric study plan was agreed with the FDA
which included a waiver for all pediatric age groups.

13 Postmarketing Requirements and Commitment
The Regulatory Authorities’ Assessment:
The following clinical pharmacology trials will be required:
PMR
1. Submit the final report and datasets from a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to determine an
appropriate starting dose of INQOVI in patients with moderate and severe hepatic
impairment that may inform product labeling. Design and conduct the trial in accordance
with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled; Pharmacokinetics in Patients with Impaired
Hepatic Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling.
2. Submit the final report and datasets from a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to determine an
appropriate starting dose of INQOVI in patients with severe renal impairment and/or endstage renal disease that may inform product labeling. Design and conduct the trial in
accordance with the FDA Guidance for Industry titled; Pharmacokinetics in Patients with
Impaired Renal Function: Study Design, Data Analysis, and Impact on Dosing and Labeling.
3. Submit the final report with datasets from a thorough QT/QTc study to evaluate the effect
of cedazuridine on QT interval prolongation that may further inform labelling. Design and
conduct the trial in accordance with the ICH E14 Guidances for Industry titled; E14 Clinical
Evaluation of QT/QTc Interval Prolongation and Proarrhythmic Potential for NonAntiarrhythmic Drugs, and E14 Clinical Evaluation of QT/QTc Interval Prolongation and
Proarrhythmic Potential for Non-Antiarrhythmic Drugs - Questions and Answers (R3).
PMC:
1. Submit the final report and datasets from a clinical pharmacokinetic trial to determine the
effect of food on decitabine and cedazuridine exposure when administered with INQOVI
that may inform product labeling. Design and conduct the trial in accordance with the FDA
Guidance for Industry titled; Food-Effect Bioavailability and Fed Bioequivalence Studies.

NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Pharmacometrics review report
19.4.4.1. SUMMARY OF FINDINGS
19.4.4.1.1. Key Review Questions
ASTX727 is a fixed dose combination of 100 mg of cedazuridine and 35 mg of decitabine.
Decitabine is the active component that is thought to act through 1) incorporation into DNA
causing apoptosis of proliferating cells and 2) inhibition of DNA methyltransferase which causes
hypomethylation and consequently promotes cellular differentiation of future cancer cells.
When taken orally, decitabine is rapidly metabolized by the liver’s cytidine deaminase resulting
in very low oral bioavailability. Cedazuridine is a potent inhibitor of cytidine deaminase and
therefore increases decitabine oral bioavailability. Renal elimination is the major route of
cedazuridine excretion. In the stomach, cedazuridine undergoes PH dependent conversion to its
epimer (a less potent cytidine deaminase inhibitor) thus decreasing bioavailability of the potent
epimer. Based on these disposition pathways, decitabine exposure is therefore dependent on
factors influencing hepatic function, renal function and stomach PH. The Applicant evaluated
potential covariates and developed population PK models that accounted for the effect of renal
impairment and sex on cedazuridine clearance and rate of absorption respectively. In turn,
cedazuridine affected decitabine hepatic clearance. The model also accounted for the effect of
body size (height) on decitabine hepatic clearance (through empirical model of body size
dependent hepatic and portal vein blood flow and volume) and sex on intravenous (IV)
decitabine hepatic clearance and central volume. However, none of these covariates had a
sufficiently large effect on clearance to justify dose adjustments. On the other hand, it is
interesting that that sex was identified as a covariate on clearance and absorption parameters
while concomitant use of proton pump inhibitors (PPI) was not identified as a covariate to
decitabine exposure. Based on this observation, the key pharmacometric review questions
were:
1. Do pharmacokinetic and exposure-vs-safety data support dose adjustment based on
measures of body size (e.g body surface area or weight)?
2. Do pharmacokinetic and exposure-vs-safety data support dose adjustment based on
measures of renal impairment (e.g. creatinine clearance[CLCR])?
3. Do pharmacokinetic data support concomittant use with proton pump inhibitors?
4. Do pharmacokinetic and exposure-vs-safety data support dose adjustment based on
biological sex?
19.4.4.1.1.1.
Do pharmacokinetic and exposure-vs-safety data support dose
adjustment based on measures of body size (e.g body surface area or weight)?
No, dose adjustment is not needed based on body surface area (BSA) or weight (WT) for
ASTX727. Although measures of body size (BSA and WT) were associated with decitabine
exposure and with proportions of patients with any grade neutropenia, prediction from logistic
regression model indicated only 5% difference in probability of any grade neutropenia between
307
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
lowest and highest BSA quartiles. This effect was not large enough to warrant dose
adjustments.
19.4.4.1.1.2.
Do pharmacokinetic and exposure-vs-safety data support dose
adjustment based on measures of renal impairment (e.g. creatinine clearance[CLCR])?
No, dose adjustment is not needed for patients with mild and moderate renal impairment.
Although renal function has a trend for association with decitabine exposure (p-value = 0.063)
and with any grade thrombocytopenia (p-value = 0.06), the effects were not statistically
significant (alpha = 0.05) to warrant dose adjustments. Furthermore, CLCR was not associated
with any grade neutropenia. ASTX727is not recommended for use in patients with severe renal
impairment, end stage renal disease and patients on dialysis since it was not studied in these
patients.
19.4.4.1.1.3.
Do pharmacokinetic data support concomittant use with proton pump
inhibitors?
Yes, ASTX727 can be used concomitantly with PPI. Although decitabine exposure was higher in
patients on concomitant PPI compared to other patients, the difference (less than 25%) does
not support avoiding concomitant used of PPI with ASTX727.
19.4.4.1.1.4.
Do pharmacokinetic and exposure-vs-safety data support dose
adjustment based on biological sex?
No, there is no need for dose adjustments based on sex. Although male subjects had about 30%
lower 5-days cumulative AUC compared to female subjects, no differences were observed when
day 1, 2, and 5 AUCs were compared between the sexes separately. Furthermore, the
association between sex and adverse events of any grade neutropenia (p-value = 0.1724 at
treatment-cycle 2) and thrombocytopenia (p-value = 0.12) were not statistically significant. In
totality, the difference between male and female AUCs and incidences of adverse events and
do not warrant dose adjustment given that ASTX727 dosing will be down-titrated in subjects
experiencing intolerable toxicity.
19.4.4.1.2. Recommendations
No dose adjustment is recommended for ASTX727 based on sex, BSA, WT or CLCR. However,
female and patients with low BSA (<1.97) and impaired renal function (CLCR < 60 ml/minor
category) should be monitored for cytopenia adverse events and dosing reduced as studied in
phase 3 clinical trial.
19.4.4.1.3. Label Statements
(12.2. Pharmacodynamics)
Based on the exposure-response analyses, a relationship between an increase in 5-day
cumulative daily decitabine exposure and a greater likelihood of some adverse reactions (e.g.,
any grade neutropenia, thrombocytopenia) was observed in clinical studies.
(12.3. Pharmacokinetics/special populations)
308
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
Decitabine exposure (AUC) increased with decreasing body surface area or body weight.
19.4.4.2. Results of Applicant’s Analysis
19.4.4.2.1. Applicants’ population pharmacokinetics results.
Data for PK analyses were obtained from phase 1 (ASTX727-01) and phase 2 (ASTX727-02)
studies that were designed to compare the pharmacokinetics of ASTX727 to that of intravenous
decitabine. In the phase 1 study, combinations of different doses of cedazuridine and
decitabine were tested to identify optimal combination with comparable exposure to 20mg/Kg
of IV decitabine. The optimal dose was compared to 20mg/Kg of IV decitabine in part-2 of the
phase 1 study (ASTX727-01) to confirm dose-comparability and treatment outcomes. The phase
2 study was a bioequivalence and treatment-outcome study comparing optimal dose tested in
phase 1B to 20mg/Kg of IV decitabine. Intensive PK sampling for characterization cedazuridine
and decitabine PK were collected. The final dataset for PK analyses consisted of 18940 PK
observations from 243 individuals. The patient population across all studies consisted of 168
males and 75 females, with a mean age of 70.3 years (with a range of 32.0-90.0 years), a mean
weight of 83.1 kg (with a range of 41.0-158 kg), a mean height of 171 cm (with a range of 144­
193 cm), a mean BSA of 1.97 m2 (with a range of 1.30-2.90 m2), a mean CrCL of 65.5
mL/min/1.73m2 (with a range of 27.7-156 mL/min/1.73m2), and a mean bilirubin of 13.2
umol/L (with a range of 3.0-51.3 umol/L)
The Applicant developed cedazuridine and decitabine population pharmacokinetics models
sequentially. A cedazuridine semi-physiologic pharmacokinetic model was developed first
(Figure 29); the estimated population PK parameters are given in Table 73. In brief, the model

(b) (4)

2.3. Reviewer’s comments
The Applicant followed appropriate pharmaco-statistical principles for model development.
However, the Applicant’s final semi-physiologic PK model for IV decitabine under-predicts Cmax
(b) (4)
and AUCs.
. Based on this, the
reviewer performed additional model evaluation to look for any model misspecification (Figure
28).
After comparing observed versus model predicted AUC the reviewer found that the Applicant’s
model unacceptably underpredicted decitabine AUC (See Figure 28). An information request was
sent to the Applicant to explain the identified underprediction of decitabine AUC. The Applicant
(b) (4)
acknowledges the underprediction and attributed it to
Due to this misspecification, the reviewer concludes that the Applicant’s IV decitabine
model failed to identify PK covariates. The Applicant did not assess impacts of covariates on oral
decitabine clearance and distributions. Estimates obtained during IV decitabine model
development were fixed when oral decitabine data were included for modeling. Based on this,
results from the Applicant’s model do not support labelling statements on dose adjustment in
renal and hepatic impaired patient nor based on body surface area. Furthermore, predictions
from the model cannot be used in exposure-vs-response or safety modeling.

NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
19.4.4.3. Reviewer’s Analysis
19.4.4.3.1. Introduction
The Applicant’s semi-physiologic PK model underpredicted observed decitabine AUC. This can
be attributed to structural model misspecifications particularly the un-estimated physiological
parameters. This misspecification may cause inaccurate identification and characterization of
effects covariates of PK parameters. Fortunately, due to intensive PK sampling, 5-day-AUC can
be calculated from observed data and used to assess PK covariates.
Furthermore, identification of factors associated with treatment related adverse events can be
extended by performing additional analyses e.g. assess factors associated with adverse events
in cycle 1 and cycle 2 separately; and to use a sequential approach to building a GLM model for
adverse events where by only potential predictors identified through univariate logistic
regression analysis are included in the final multivariate model.
19.4.4.3.2. Objectives
The reviewer performed additional PK and exposure-safety analyses to:
Identify intrinsic and extrinsic factors associated with decitabine AUC using intensively collected
PK data
Identify predictors of neutropenia and thrombocytopenia in each treatment cycle separately.
19.4.4.3.3. Methods
19.4.4.3.3.1.
Data Sets
Datasets for PK and exposure-safety analyses were constructed from ADAM datasets supplied
by the Applicant i.e. adsl.xpt, lb.xpt, pc.xpt, and adae.xpt. For preparation of PK datasets,
records on intensive PK sampling on day 1, 2 and 5 from both phase 1B (ASTX727-01-B) and
phase 2 (ASTX727-02) studies were identified and used to calculate AUC between 0 – 8 hours
(AUC0-8) for each day. AUCs were calculated using the linear trapezoidal method. For the
decitabine PK dataset, cumulative five-day AUC was calculated by multiplying average of day 2
and day 5 AUC by 5 (5-day-AUC = (day2-AUC+day5-AUC)/2) while for IV decitabine 5-day-AUC
was calculated by multiplying average of day 1 and day 5 AUC by 5 (5-day-AUC = (day1­
AUC+day5-AUC)/2). Individuals with less than 5 PK observation in a day were removed from the
dataset due to potentially unreliable AUC. Covariate information were obtained from adsl.xpt
and lb.xpt datasets. Creatine clearance (CLCR) and estimated glomerular filtration rates (eGFR)
were calculated using Cockcroft-Gault and MDRD equations respectively.
For exposure-safety datasets, dependent variables were identified from the AEHLT variable in
the adae.xpt datasets. The NCYCSTD variable was used to identify treatment cycles, and 3
separate adverse events datasets were prepared for each treatment cycle. The pc.xpt datasets
provided PK data for calculation of 5-day-AUC. The exposure and covariate datasets were
joined to adverse events datasets to complete construction of analysis datasets.

318
Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

NDA Multi-disciplinary Review and Evaluation
NDA 212576, Original
INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
19.4.4.3.3.2.
Software
The R software for statistical computing (Version 3.5.3) was used for preparation of the analysis
datasets, for graphical analyses and for linear and generalize linear modeling
19.4.4.3.3.3.
Models
Univariate and multivariate linear and logistic regression models were used in the analyses.
Before such analyses, collinearities among independent variables were assessed. In univariate
analyses one independent variable was assessed for association with a dependent variable. For
PK analysis, 5-day-AUC was log transformed to create a dependent variable. Independent
variables with univariate p-values of < 0.05 were included in the multivariate analysis regardless
of collinearity among them to control for possible confounding effect. For exposure-safety
analyses, variables which were collinear with AUC were not included in the multivariate
analyses.

Version date: June 11, 2019 (ALL NDA/ BLA reviews)
Reference ID: 4636063

INQOVI (decitabine and cedazuridine)
19.4.4.3.4. Results
19.4.4.3.4.1.
Extrinsic and intrinsic factors associated with decitabine AUC
Figure 30 shows results from collinearity analyses among independent variables.
Graphical exploration and univariate analyses indicated that age, sex, weight, height, CLCR, and
BSA were associated with oral decitabine 5-day-AUCs at p-values < 0.05. A multivariate analysis
identified both sex (p-value = 0.000082) and BSA (p-value = 0.020) to be the independent
predictors of 5-day-AUC for oral decitabine (See Table 77). Table 77 indicates that male
subjects (represented by intercept parameter) with age of 70 years, BSA of 1.73 m2 and CLCR of
90 mil/min would have about 30% lower 5-days-AUC compared to female patients of the same
age, BSA, and CLCR.

associated with decitabine daily AUC after adjusting for sex, weight and height.

Concomitant use of proton pump inhibitors was assessed as a potential extrinsic factor
impacting decitabine exposure. Data for concomitant drug use was obtained from cm.xpt
dataset. The CMENRTPT variable was used to identify patients who were on ongoing PPI use
and those who were not. Graphical and linear model analysis was used to determine if ongoing
PPI used has impact on decitabine exposure. Figure 31 and Table 79 shows that subjects on
ongoing PPI use in the phase 2 study had 23.3% higher decitabine 5-day-AUC (P-value=0.009)
compared to subjects not on PPI. Despite this significant trend the effect is considered small
and is not consistent with results from the phase 1 study

19.4.4.3.4.2.
Factors associated with any grade neutropenias in phase 1B and 2 studies
Graphical analyses for the relationship between any grade neutropenias in treatment-cycle
1 and different patient characteristics are given in Figure 32 and Figure 33. In univariate
General Linear Model (GLM) analyses, none of the patient characteristics were associated
with likelihood of any grade neutropenias.
Figure 34 and Figure 35, show results from graphical analyses for the relationship between
any grade neutropenias in treatment-cycle 2 with different patient characteristics. Female
subjects appear to higher proportions of any grade neutropenias compared to male
subjects. However, in univariate GLM, sex was not a predictor of any grade neutropenias (p­
value = 0.172), only day-5-AUC was associated with likelihood of any grade neutropenias (p­
value = 0.0187). Nevertheless, in a multivariate GLM, independent variables included logtransformed 5-days-AUC, sex, and study (phase 1 or 2), treatment-sequence (Oral->IV or IV­
>oral) and interaction term between study and sequence. Table 80 shows results from the
multivariate logistic regression analysis. The multivariate analysis indicate that the
likelihood of any grade neutropenia increased with increasing 5-days-AUC. Furthermore,
subjects in phase 2 study had more likelihood of any grade neutropenias than in phase 1.
Similarly, in treatment-cycle 2, subjects in phase 2 and in oral->IV sequence (i.e. receiving IV
decitabine in cycle 2) had less likelihood of neutropenias compared to other
study/sequence interactions.
Figure 36 shows the proportions of patients with any grade neutropenias in treatment-cycle
3 for different categories of subject characteristics. The figure shows decreasing proportion
of neutropenia with increasing body-surface area, and lower proportion with neutropenia in
male subjects compared to female. In contrast, proportions of neutropenias seem to
increase with higher 5-days-AUC and higher baseline neutrophil counts. However, in
univariate analyses, none of the covariates are associated with neutropenia in treatment­
cycle3 (p-values of 0.125, 0.449, 0.229, 0.165, for 5-days-AUC, BSA, baseline neutrophils,
and sex). A multivariate analysis with 5-days-AUC, baseline neutrophils, study (phase 1 or 2),
treatment-sequence (Oral->IV or IV->oral) and interaction term between study and
sequence had a better fit than any other combination of independent variables. Table 81
shows parameters of the multivariate model while Figure 37 shows good agreement
between observed and predicted proportions of neutropenia in different quantiles of 5­
days-AUC and BSA for treatment cycle 3.
In conclusion, the results from these additional analyses do not warrant dose reduction
based on the assessed patient factors.

19.4.4.3.4.3.
Factors associated with any grade thrombocytopenias in phase 1 and 2
studies
Graphical and univariate GLM identified some covariates with trend for association with any
grade thrombocytopenias in treatment cycles 1 and 2 (p-value > 0.05 and p-value <=0.20).
Table 82 and Figure 38 show results from univariate GLM and graphical analyses
respectively for treatment-cycle 1, while Table 83 and Figure 39 shows similar results for
treatment cycle 2. In conclusion, despite differences in 5-days-AUC based on BSA and sex,
incidences of thrombocytopenia was not associated with these covariates. Therefore, dose
adjustment is not necessary based on these factors.
